# Content review record

One section per protocol. Every number in a protocol is listed with where it was checked. "Checked twice" means it was read when drafting and confirmed again from the source after drafting (second pass, 2026-11-16/17).

Source keys used below:
- AHA-ABLS: American Heart Association, Part 7: Adult Basic Life Support, 2025 AHA Guidelines for CPR and ECC, Circulation 2025;152(suppl 2):S448-S478. https://cpr.heart.org/en/resuscitation-science/cpr-and-ecc-guidelines/adult-basic-life-support (full text also read at https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/full/10.1161/CIR.0000000000001369)
- AHA-PBLS: AHA and American Academy of Pediatrics, Part 6: Pediatric Basic Life Support, 2025, Circulation 2025;152(suppl 2):S424-S447. https://cpr.heart.org/en/resuscitation-science/cpr-and-ecc-guidelines/pediatric-basic-life-support (full text also read at https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/full/10.1161/CIR.0000000000001370)
- AHA-SPEC: AHA, Part 10: Adult and Pediatric Special Circumstances of Resuscitation, 2025. https://cpr.heart.org/en/resuscitation-science/cpr-and-ecc-guidelines/adult-and-pediatric-special-circumstances-of-resuscitation
- AHA-HL: AHA, Highlights of the 2025 Guidelines for CPR and ECC. https://cpr.heart.org/-/media/CPR-Files/2025-documents-for-cpr-heart-edits-posting/Resuscitation-Science/252500_Hghlghts_2025ECCGuidelines.pdf
- AHA-FA: AHA and American Red Cross, 2024 Guidelines for First Aid, Circulation 2024;150:e519. https://cpr.heart.org/en/resuscitation-science/2024-first-aid-guidelines (the current joint first aid edition; the older cpr.heart.org "First Aid Guidelines" landing page still shows 2020 text)
- ARC-DB: American Red Cross Scientific Advisory Council, First Aid Guidelines database (pages modified Oct-Nov 2025). https://firstaidguide.wpenginepowered.com/ (linked from https://www.redcross.org/take-a-class/resources/guidelines)
- ARC-LAY: American Red Cross lay "learn first aid" / CPR / AED pages on redcross.org (read 2026-11-16).
- STB: American College of Surgeons, STOP THE BLEED course, lay public presentation, version 2 (2019). https://www.stopthebleed.org/media/omalhrif/lay-public-stop-the-bleed-presentation-pdf.pdf
- TCCC: Joint Trauma System / CoTCCC, TCCC Guidelines 2026 (focused update of 2024). https://books.allogy.com/web/tenant/8/books/b729b76a-1a34-4bf7-b76b-66bb2072b2a7/

## Open questions for Jordan

- **Improvised tourniquet width (bleeding).** AHA-FA (2024, 9.1, Class 2a C-EO) says at least 2 inches wide; ARC-DB (Improvised tourniquets, 2025 update) says at least 1.5 inches. Used AHA (higher authority): 2 inches.
- **Open chest wound cover (chest-wound).** AHA-FA 2024 (9.2) and ARC-DB: reasonable to leave the wound open to air or cover with a clean non-sealing dry dressing; vented chest seal only by trained responders; routine chest seal by untrained lay responders not recommended. TCCC 2026: every open/sucking chest wound gets a vented chest seal immediately, and an unvented seal if no vented one is available. Used AHA/Red Cross for the main path; the protocol offers a vented seal only to trained users with one, explains the TCCC position in the detail, and does not tell lay users to use an unvented seal. If your group carries vented seals and is TCCC-trained, you may want the seal path to be the default.
- **Infant chest thrust technique (choking-infant).** AHA-PBLS 2025 uses the heel of one hand for infant chest thrusts (the two-finger technique was dropped for CPR compressions and replaced for thrusts). The Red Cross lay infant choking page (read 2026-11-16) still shows two fingers in the centre of the chest just below the nipple line. Used AHA: heel of one hand. Red Cross gives the depth (about 1.5 inches) and the positioning, which AHA does not contradict; kept those.
- **Choking sequence edition (choking, choking-infant).** Recorded for clarity: AHA 2025 (AHA-ABLS, AHA-PBLS, AHA-HL) starts adults and children with 5 back blows alternating with 5 abdominal thrusts; earlier AHA editions started with abdominal thrusts. Red Cross lay page also uses 5 back blows and 5 abdominal thrusts. No disagreement now; noted because users trained before 2025 on AHA may expect abdominal thrusts first.
- **Second epinephrine dose timing (anaphylaxis).** ARC-LAY: second dose if signs persist 5 to 10 minutes after the first and EMS has not arrived. ARC-DB: second dose may be considered for continued signs within 5 to 15 minutes. AHA-FA: repeat dose may be considered if no response and EMS will be more than 5 to 10 minutes. Used 5 to 10 minutes (AHA and Red Cross lay agree); the timer is set to 5 minutes as the earliest point. Off-grid, EMS will always be more than 10 minutes away, so in practice the second dose is given at 5 to 10 minutes if signs continue.
- **Discoloured auto-injector (anaphylaxis).** ARC-DB says a cloudy or discoloured solution should be discarded. It does not say what to do when it is the only dose available in the field. The protocol states the Red Cross position only. You may want medical advice on this for your group.
- **Spine injury with a compromised airway (recovery-position).** Sources say leave a possible spine injury as found unless moving is needed for safety, CPR or bleeding control. They do not address an unresponsive trauma casualty who is breathing but vomiting. The protocol does not invent an answer; it says to roll for CPR if breathing stops. Consider asking a medical adviser.
- **Lone rescuer with no signal (CPR child/infant) — updated after review.** The AHA 2025 Pediatric BLS single-rescuer algorithm (accessible long-description PDF, https://cpr.heart.org/-/media/CPR-Files/CPR-Guidelines-Files/2025-Accessible/Algorithm-BLS-Ped-Single-Rescuer-LngDscrp-250618-Ed.pdf, Boxes 5, 5a and 7) covers infants and children: if the sudden collapse was witnessed, activate the emergency response and get the AED first; otherwise give CPR, and after 2 minutes, if still alone, activate the emergency response and get the AED. ARC-LAY says the same for babies. Both cpr-child and cpr-infant now state this, attributed to AHA. Off-grid "activate" can mean leaving for hours; the protocols state the AHA rule and the trade-off and leave the decision to the rescuer. Which should the group follow?
- **Adult lone rescuer with no signal (cpr-adult).** AHA-ABLS 2025 (Class 1, B-NR): a lone responder should activate the emergency response system first, then immediately begin CPR; supporting text adds that local circumstances decide whether to delay CPR to activate EMS. The first version told users to start CPR straight away. It now states AHA's advice and the off-grid trade-off (help may be hours away) and leaves the choice to the user. Which should the group follow off-grid?
- **Second-dose frequency (anaphylaxis).** AHA-FA 2024 6.5 supportive text: 7% to 18% of people with anaphylaxis need more than one dose. ARC-DB: 18 to 35 percent. Used AHA (higher authority).
- **Possible spine injury found face down (recovery-position).** ARC-LAY (unresponsive and breathing): with a suspected head, neck or back injury, leave in the position found unless moving for safety, CPR or bleeding. ILCOR 2026 CoSTR FA 7040 (good-practice statement) and ARC-DB Positioning: people found face down, prone, or in neck or torso flexion should be repositioned supine for reassessment. The protocol states both in s1's detail; the spine branch keeps "leave as found". Which should apply?
- **Tourniquet time on long evacuations (bleeding).** TCCC 2026: reassess every tourniquet; convert to a hemostatic or pressure dressing within 2 hours where the casualty is not in shock, the wound can be watched, and it is not an amputation; ASM/CLS personnel should not convert beyond 2 hours unless directed; do not remove after 6 hours without monitoring and lab capability. Off-grid evacuations will often exceed 2 hours and the protocol says only "leave it on". Should the group be trained in conversion (Advanced procedures)?
- **Abdominal and other torso wounds (bleeding).** The bleeding protocol now sends belly and other body wounds to firm direct pressure only (AHA-FA 9.1 Class 1 direct pressure). ARC-DB's bleeding algorithm lists "wound packing, if trained" for head, neck and trunk wounds; STB v2 lists packing locations without excluding the body. Should the group pack a heavily bleeding belly wound, or press only?
- **Adult chest-thrust hand position (choking) — round 2.** No AHA or Red Cross page that could be opened gives a hand position (AHA-ABLS cpr.heart.org page and the AHA adult FBAO algorithm give indications only; ARC-LAY gives indications only; the AHA full text on ahajournals could not be reached). The technique in choking s8 now comes from MedlinePlus (US National Library of Medicine, https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000047.htm, review date 2026-02-17), which is outside the AHA/Red Cross tier: arms around the chest, fist on the middle of the breastbone between the nipples, firm backward thrusts. Mayo Clinic (https://www.mayoclinic.org/first-aid/first-aid-choking/basics/art-20056637, not on the authority list) gives a lower landmark: hands at the base of the breastbone, just above the joining of the lowest ribs. Which landmark should the group train?
- **Chest-thrust trigger in pregnancy (choking).** AHA-ABLS 2025: chest thrusts in the "late stages of pregnancy". ARC-LAY: anyone "obviously pregnant or known to be pregnant" (a broader trigger). The step states both. Which should the group use?
- **Spine injury with a compromised airway (recovery-position, spine-injury) — Task 11 addition.** MedlinePlus Spinal injury (https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000029.htm, reviewed 2025-06-04, outside the AHA/Red Cross tier) answers part of the earlier question: roll a person with a possible spine injury only if they are vomiting or choking on blood, or to check breathing; use at least two people, one holding the head, and turn them as one unit keeping head, neck and back in line. AHA-FA 2024 9.4 (stay as still as possible unless safety warrants movement) and the Red Cross lay page (unless moving for CPR or bleeding control) do not mention vomiting. spine-injury s3/s4 now use the MedlinePlus technique, attributed. MedlinePlus also says CPR on a possible spine injury should be compressions only without moving the neck; AHA includes breaths and the cpr protocols allow a jaw thrust. The spine protocol states both. Is the MedlinePlus roll acceptable to you as the group's method?
- **Straightening a broken limb with poor circulation (fracture).** AHA-FA 2024 9.7 (Class 2b, C-EO): treat a deformed broken limb in the position found unless straightening is needed for safe and prompt transport; no evidence that first aid providers can reduce fractures safely. Both MedlinePlus pages (outside the tier) give the same exception. Broken bone: if circulation is poor and trained personnel are not quickly available, try to realign the limb into a normal resting position. How to make a splint: do not change the position of, or realign, an injured part, but if medical help is not available and the part looks abnormally bent and the limb is pale, gently placing it back in its normal position may improve circulation (both re-read 2026-11-17, round 2). fracture s8 states both, and its action text and warn exclude hip, pelvis and thigh injuries (MedlinePlus Broken bone: do not move these unless absolutely necessary). fracture s8 states AHA first, then the MedlinePlus off-grid option. Should the group be trained to realign a pulseless limb, or follow AHA strictly?
- **Cold on a sprain: 20 to 30 minutes or 20 on, 20 off (sprain-dislocation, fracture).** AHA-FA 2024 9.6: limit cold to 20 to 30 minutes per application (supportive text: 3 to 4 times a day). Red Cross lay pages (Fractures, Muscle Bone and Joint Injury, Wounds): no more than 20 minutes, then wait 20 minutes; 10 on/10 off if 20 minutes is not tolerated. The timers are set to 20 minutes (inside both); sprain-dislocation states both; fracture states the Red Cross rule (AHA 9.7 says nothing about cold on fractures).
- **Ice on burns (burns).** AHA-FA 2024 9.8 (Class 2b, C-LD): if clean running water is unavailable, superficial burns with intact skin may be cooled with ice wrapped in cloth, for no more than 10 minutes. Red Cross lay Burns page: do not use ice ("may further damage the skin"); use a cold, non-freezing compress. burns s5 gives the Red Cross compress first and the AHA ice option with its limits.
- **Petroleum jelly on a burn (burns).** AHA-FA 2024 9.8 (Class 2b, B-R): after cooling, small partial-thickness burns managed at home may be dressed with petrolatum, petrolatum-based antibiotic ointment, honey or aloe under a non-stick dressing. Red Cross lay Burns page: do not put butter, mayonnaise or petroleum jelly on a burn (traps heat). burns s8 says no grease on a fresh burn and gives the AHA option for later care of small burns, attributed.
- **Chemical in the eye: how long to flush (eye-injury, burns).** AHA-FA 2024 8.3 (Class 1, C-LD): 15 minutes of tap water. MedlinePlus Eye emergencies: at least 15 minutes. Red Cross lay Eye Injury and Burns pages: keep flushing until EMS arrives. Off-grid EMS may be hours away. The timer is 15 minutes; eye-injury s2 states all three.
- **Covering both eyes (eye-injury).** MedlinePlus Eye emergencies: loosely bandage both eyes for an embedded object, and cover both eyes if blood pools in the eye, to limit eye movement. TCCC CLS Module 05 (2020): do not cover both eyes unless both are injured (military reason: the casualty can keep functioning). AHA-FA 9.10 says only to tape a shield or cup over the eye. eye-injury s5 states both.
- **Tourniquet for every limb amputation (amputation).** TCCC 2026 TFC 3a: apply a limb tourniquet for any traumatic amputation. MedlinePlus Amputation - traumatic: do not use a tourniquet unless bleeding is life-threatening. MedlinePlus also says to raise the injured area, which AHA-FA 9.1 and ARC say does not help (bleeding protocol don'ts). amputation s2 uses TCCC (higher on the authority list) for arm or leg amputations and direct pressure for fingers and toes, and leaves out elevation.
- **Impaled object exceptions (impaled-object).** The task asked for the exceptions the source gives to "leave it in place". None of the sources that could be opened give any: Red Cross lay (Life-threatening external bleeding, embedded objects) and MedlinePlus (Cuts and puncture wounds) say only do not remove. EMS and military teaching often allows removal when the object blocks the airway or prevents CPR, but no source for that was opened, so the protocol gives no exception. Do you want one, and from which source?
- **Antibiotic ointment on wounds (wound-care).** AHA-FA 2024 9.3 supportive text: antibiotic or antibacterial dressings have not been shown to improve healing or reduce infection in clean wounds. WMS 2014 update (grade 2C): topical antibiotics may help, with little downside in people who are not allergic. MedlinePlus: apply antibacterial ointment. wound-care s7 states all three without choosing.
- **Draining blisters (blisters).** WMS 2014 update (grade 1C): drain blisters larger than 5 mm but leave the roof on. NHS Blisters (UK, not on the authority list): do not burst a blister yourself; a doctor may drain a large or painful one with a sterilised needle. blisters s3 follows WMS and states the NHS position. No source opened describes how a lay person should drain one; the step says sterilised needle (from the NHS description of what a doctor does) and leaves the roof in place.
- **Sleep after a concussion when no provider can check them (head-injury).** Red Cross Concussion: sleeping is generally considered safe once a health care provider has checked the person; the provider may recommend periodic waking. CDC: being very drowsy or impossible to wake is a danger sign. No source gives a waking schedule for someone who has not been checked. head-injury s8 lets them sleep with someone watching and treats difficulty waking as a danger sign. Do you want a waking interval, and from which medical adviser?
- **CPR in traumatic cardiac arrest (march).** AHA: CPR for anyone unresponsive and not breathing normally. TCCC 2026 TFC 17: battlefield CPR for blast or penetrating trauma with no pulse, no breathing and no signs of life will not succeed and should not be attempted; torso or polytrauma casualties first get bilateral needle decompression. march s16 sends the user to CPR and states the TCCC position. Off-grid, which should the group follow?
- **MARCH source level (march).** The newest All Service Members (ASM) curriculum document that could be opened is the 2019 ASM Rapid Casualty Assessment skill sheet; the TCCC 2026 guidelines no longer name the MARCH sequence or an ASM tier (they refer to ASM/CLS personnel). march follows the 2019 ASM sheet for the order and lay-level actions and TCCC 2026 for the clinical content. If a newer ASM course is in use, it should be compared.
- **Cleaning major wounds (wound-care) — Task 11 review.** MedlinePlus Cuts and puncture wounds: "Do not try to clean a major wound, especially after the bleeding is under control." WMS 2014 irrigates wilderness wounds with at least 1 L under pressure. wound-care now stops at s10/s11 for any wound that needed a tourniquet, packing or a pressure bandage (leave it in place, do not clean, cover, evacuate) and irrigates only minor and moderate wounds. Should a deep wound that needed packing or a pressure dressing ever be cleaned on a long off-grid evacuation?
- **Vomiting as a danger sign after a head injury (head-injury).** Red Cross Concussion lists nausea or vomiting as needing immediate emergency care and says to call 911 for vomiting. CDC and AHA say repeated vomiting. Off-grid, does a single vomit trigger evacuation?
- **Pain medicine in burn casualties (burns).** AHA 9.8 (2a): OTC analgesics (acetaminophen or NSAIDs) for burn pain, no exclusions stated. Red Cross: no painkillers after a head injury. Shock protocol: nothing by mouth. burns s9 now limits pain medicine to small burns, with a warn excluding shock, burns needing evacuation, possible head injury and reduced alertness. The reviewer noted TCCC practice of avoiding NSAIDs other than meloxicam in casualties; the TCCC 2026 analgesia section (re-read 2026-11-17) lists only meloxicam among NSAIDs and does not state an avoidance rule, so no NSAID exclusion for bleeding is in the app. Should one be added?
- **Stopping burn cooling for a shivering child (burns).** AHA 9.8 (Class 1): monitor preadolescent children for hypothermia; Red Cross: be alert for hypothermia. Neither gives a stop rule. burns s5 now says "watch children for shivering" and the detail gives stop, dry and warm at the first shivering as a stated judgement. Acceptable as the group rule?
- **Lone rescuer and a vomiting casualty with a possible spine injury (spine-injury, recovery-position).** MedlinePlus Spinal injury and Mayo Clinic both require at least two people for the roll. Neither (re-read 2026-11-17) covers a lone rescuer. The protocols do not invent a one-person technique. What should a lone rescuer do?
- **Impaled-object exceptions — update (impaled-object).** Oregon EMS Psychomotor Skills Lab Manual (2023, EMS level, not on the authority list): removal allowed when the object obstructs the airway so ventilations are ineffective; fishhooks if local protocols allow. Now stated in impaled-object s2 detail, attributed. MedlinePlus Fishhook removal (reviewed 2025-10-14): a lay person may remove a hook only just under the skin; not if deep or in or near an eye, eyelid, joint, tendon or artery. impaled-object now has a fishhook branch (s6, s7, s8) using MedlinePlus. No source found for a CPR-interference exception. Adopt the airway exception for the group?
- **Blister draining trigger (blisters) — update.** WMS 2014: drain blisters over 5 mm, keep the roof. American Academy of Dermatology (updated 2026-07-06): drain only a blister that is large and very painful; technique: needle wiped with rubbing alcohol, pierce one edge, leave the roof, wash, petroleum jelly, loose cover, doughnut pad on the sole. NHS: do not burst it yourself. blisters s3 uses the WMS threshold and the AAD technique. Which trigger should the group use?
- **Breathing and pulse check in a very cold casualty (hypothermia, frostbite).** WMS hypothermia 2019 (Grade 1C): feel for a carotid pulse for 1 minute; if none, start CPR with rescue breathing. AHA 2025 Part 10 section 17: pulse and breathing may be very slow and hard to detect. The AHA lay-rescuer rule used in the CPR protocols is no pulse check and a quick look at breathing. hypothermia s1 and s15 tell the user to watch and feel for breathing for up to 1 minute (the WMS time, applied to breathing for a lay user) before deciding; the pulse check is in the detail. Is a 1-minute check acceptable for the group, or should lay users go straight to CPR?
- **Where to put heat packs (hypothermia).** WMS 2019 (1B): armpits, chest and back, in that order; never the limbs. TCCC 2026 (march s13): front of the chest and armpits. CDC Preventing Hypothermia: centre of the body, chest, neck, head and groin. AHA 2024: none on the limbs, no site named. hypothermia s6/s13 use WMS (consistent with TCCC) and mention CDC in the detail.
- **Frostbite rewarming water (frostbite).** AHA 2024 7.8 (Class 1) and the Red Cross lay steps: 99 to 104 F (37 to 40 C); studies used 20 to 30 minutes (AHA). The same Red Cross page's FAQ: not more than 100 to 105 F (38 to 40.5 C). MedlinePlus Frostbite: 104 to 108 F (40 to 42.2 C) for 15 to 30 minutes. WMS 2019 (AAFP 2020 summary and Medical Dialogues 2019 summary; the WMS 2024 full text could not be opened): 98.6 to 102.2 F (37 to 39 C), kept in range with a thermometer; without one, an uninjured extremity in the water for 30 seconds; rewarming typically complete within 30 minutes. Review round 1 (controller ruling): frostbite s9 now uses the overlap of AHA and WMS, 99 to 102 F (37 to 39 C), so neither is contradicted; the warning keeps AHA's harm figure (113 F (45 C) harmful in animal studies). Timer 30 minutes, stop when soft and colour returns. Which range should the group use?
- **Rewarming frostbite yourself (frostbite).** MedlinePlus How to prevent frostbite and hypothermia: never rewarm frostbite on your own. AHA 2024 and Red Cross: rewarm in lukewarm water at the earliest opportunity if it cannot refreeze. Used AHA; off-grid there is usually no one else to do it.
- **Frostnip (frostbite).** No AHA, Red Cross or CDC page that could be opened defines frostnip; the definition and care (red, cold skin that may go white but stays soft; warm against skin; do not rub) are from MedlinePlus (outside the top tier). Update (review round 1): WMS 2019 also defines frostnip, as a superficial non-freezing cold injury of exposed skin (AAFP 2020 summary); frostbite s4 now cites it.
- **Ibuprofen for frostbite (frostbite).** AHA 2024 7.8 (Class 2b, C-EO): ibuprofen may be reasonable to prevent tissue damage and treat pain; no dose and no exclusions given. frostbite s11 gives it "as the package directs" with the app's by-mouth exclusions in the warning. WMS 2019 (AAFP summary) gives a field dose (up to 600 mg four times daily); not used, because the guide allows a dose only as the AHA/Red Cross guideline states it. Acceptable?
- **Heat stroke evacuation (heat-illness).** AHA 2024 7.9 (Class 1): activate emergency services for heat stroke. WMS 2024 (magazine summary): a healthy person with exertional heat stroke who returns to normal mental state and mobility within 2 hours of treatment may be released to self-care; non-exertional heat stroke always goes to hospital. heat-illness s8 and the evacuate list follow AHA (evacuate even if they recover) and state the WMS position. Which should the group follow?
- **Ice-pack placement (heat-illness).** WMS 2024 summary: if packs cannot cover the whole body, cheeks, palms and soles (about twice as fast as neck, armpits and groin, which showed no benefit). CDC/NIOSH Heat-related Illnesses: ice or cold wet cloths on the head, neck, armpits and groin. AHA 2024: alternatives are slower than immersion but faster than ice packs to the armpits and groin. heat-illness s6 uses WMS and states NIOSH.
- **How long to cool without immersion (heat-illness) — off-grid departure.** Red Cross Heat Stroke: continue cooling for 20 minutes, or until their condition improves or EMS personnel arrive. Off-grid, EMS will not arrive. heat-illness s6 keeps cooling until the person is thinking clearly (timer 20 minutes, then the ask), and s9 keeps cooling all the way out if they are still confused. AHA 2024 immersion: 15 minutes or until neurological symptoms resolve, whichever comes first. Re-cooling when confusion returns (s7) is inferred from the AHA definition of heat stroke; no source states it. Acceptable?
- **Sugar in rehydration drinks (dehydration).** AHA 2024 7.10 (2a): a 4 to 9% carbohydrate-electrolyte drink is reasonable over water. Red Cross Dehydration: avoid sugary, caffeinated or alcoholic drinks. CDC Yellow Book: overly sweet drinks (sodas) can cause osmotic diarrhoea in quantity. The homemade recipe (WHO EMRO, undated public leaflet WHO-EM/CSR/594/E): 1 litre clean water, 6 teaspoons sugar, half a teaspoon salt; WHO does not say level or heaped. dehydration s6 gives the WHO recipe. No AHA or Red Cross page gives a recipe. Do you want the recipe in the app?
- **Pain medicine for altitude headache (altitude).** CDC Yellow Book 2026 lists over-the-counter headache medicines with doses for altitude headache. The content guide allows over-the-counter pain medicine only where the AHA/Red Cross first aid guideline recommends it, and AHA/Red Cross do not cover altitude. altitude gives no pain medicine. Prescription altitude drugs are mentioned only as "take them as their prescription says". Do you want OTC headache medicine added?
- **Pulse check after a lightning strike (lightning).** CDC Lightning first aid: check for breathing and a pulse (carotid or femoral). WMS 2012 (superseded): check for a central pulse before CPR, because temporary paralysis can make limbs pulseless. AHA lay rule (CPR protocols): no pulse check. lightning s5 asks only about responsiveness and breathing, and sends to the CPR protocols.
- **Lightning source edition (lightning).** The WMS lightning guideline 2014 update (Wilderness Environ Med 2014;25:S86-S95) full text could not be opened (wemjournal.org client error; the wmsdev mirror is blocked to automated fetching). The protocol uses WMS's own summary of the 2014 update (wms.org PDF, Engeln), CDC, and AHA 2025; the superseded 2012 full text (mirror) is used only for the reverse-triage rationale and removing metal objects. Should someone check the 2014 full text?
- **Rescue breaths in the water (drowning).** MedlinePlus Non-fatal drowning: begin rescue breathing as soon as you can, even before reaching land. WMS 2024 summary: in-water resuscitation only by trained rescuers with suitable equipment, in safe conditions, when the shore is far; otherwise extract immediately. AHA 2025 section 11.2 shows a mismatched recommendation (the table text is about ECG filtering), so its in-water position could not be confirmed. drowning s1 follows WMS.
- **Abdominal thrusts after drowning (drowning).** WMS 2024: not recommended at any time. MedlinePlus: not unless repeated attempts to open the airway and give rescue breaths have failed and you think the airway is blocked. drowning s4: no thrusts to expel water; if a solid object may be blocking the airway, use the Choking, adult and child protocol.
- **Vomiting during CPR after drowning (drowning).** No source that could be opened gives a technique. WMS 2024 warns only about aspiration risk. drowning s4 says turn them on their side so it drains, clear vomit you can see from the mouth, roll back and continue CPR (side position from the Recovery position protocol's rationale; the CPR protocols say look in the mouth and remove what you can see). Acceptable?
- **Drowning release after observation (drowning).** WMS 2024: release may be considered only if there are no symptoms other than a mild cough and the lungs sound normal through a stethoscope; ideally someone stays for 6 hours. MedlinePlus: anyone who needed any resuscitation, even rescue breathing alone, goes to hospital. drowning sends anyone who needed breaths or CPR or was unresponsive to evacuation (MedlinePlus), and others to a 6-hour watch (WMS); it notes that most groups cannot listen to the lungs. No 6-hour timer was added because a step timer stops when the protocol is closed.
- **Stopping CPR after cold-water drowning (drowning, hypothermia).** WMS drowning 2024: stopping might be reasonable after more than 30 minutes under water warmer than 6 C (43 F), more than 90 minutes in colder water, or after 25 minutes of continuous CPR. WMS hypothermia 2019: continue resuscitation regardless of core temperature; recoveries after 6.5 hours of CPR. drowning s3 gives the WMS drowning criteria and points to the Hypothermia protocol for a cold casualty. Which should apply when both fit?
- **Carbon monoxide: snow shelters and the Poison Help line (carbon-monoxide).** CPSC names tents, campers and vehicles as enclosed spaces where fuel-burning heaters and lanterns must not run during sleep; carbon-monoxide s6 adds snow shelters as an enclosed space (no opened source names them). MedlinePlus gives the US Poison Help phone number; it is left out because a phone number in step text may be turned into a dial link by iOS.
- **Field rewarming threshold (frostbite) — review round 1.** WMS 2019 (AAFP 2020 and Medical Dialogues 2019 summaries): rapid rewarming in the field only if definitive care is more than 2 hours away and refreezing can be prevented; otherwise let it thaw spontaneously, or keep it frozen until a thawed state can be kept. AHA 2024: rewarm at the earliest opportunity if there is no risk of refreezing. frostbite s8b detail states the WMS threshold; the protocol follows AHA. Is the 2-hour threshold relevant off-grid?
- **Walking out on thawed feet (frostbite) — review round 1.** WMS 2019 (Medical Dialogues summary): avoid walking on a thawed foot unless only the tips of the toes are affected. WMS 2019 (AAFP summary): a frozen limb should not be used for walking or climbing until care is reached. AHA 2024: avoid walking on frozen feet when possible. New frostbite s8b asks "Will they have to walk out on frostbitten feet afterwards, with nobody able to carry them?"; Yes goes to s8c (do not rewarm the feet before the walk out; pad and protect; rewarm once they can be carried or reach care). Walking on frozen feet is itself discouraged by AHA, so this is a trade-off. Should the group keep feet frozen for a walk out?
- **Frostbite blisters (frostbite) — review round 1.** WMS 2019 (AAFP summary): draining clear blisters and leaving blood-filled ones intact is common hospital practice, with insufficient evidence to recommend it. AHA 2024 (3: No Benefit): first aid providers should not debride frostbite blisters. Red Cross: do not break them. The protocol follows AHA and Red Cross (leave blisters alone).
- **Heat stroke still confused after 15 minutes of immersion (heat-illness) — review round 1.** AHA 2024 and Red Cross cap immersion at 15 minutes or until thinking is normal, whichever comes first; neither says what to do next. New heat-illness s5 ask "Are they thinking clearly now?"; No goes to s5b (take them out, keep cooling by soaking and fanning, evacuate urgently) and then s9. This is a judgement. Acceptable?
- **Stop rule for cooling without immersion over a long evacuation (heat-illness s9) — review round 1.** No opened source gives a field stop rule without a thermometer. Red Cross: continue cooling for 20 minutes, or until their condition improves or EMS personnel arrive. s9 now says stop when they are thinking clearly or start shivering, then watch and re-cool if confusion returns (labelled a judgement in the detail). Acceptable?
- **Exercise-associated hyponatremia (heat-illness) — review round 1.** WMS heat 2024 summary says only to drink to thirst and that EAH may present like heat exhaustion. heat-illness s8 and s11 warnings now say: if they have drunk large amounts of plain water and are getting worse, give no more plain water and evacuate. Management (fluid restriction, salty food) is in the separate WMS EAH guideline, which was not opened. Keep this warning?
- **Sleeping medicines at altitude (altitude) — review round 1.** CDC Yellow Book 2026: avoid alcohol for the first 48 hours; respiratory depressants such as alcohol and opiates should not be used to aid sleep; short half-life hypnotics (zolpidem 5 mg, zaleplon 5 mg) are generally safe. The first draft banned all sleeping pills and credited CDC. altitude s5 and the don't now give CDC's alcohol and breathing-depressant advice, and separately, labelled as a group caution and not a CDC rule: do not take any sedating medicine to sleep unless a prescriber has said it is safe at altitude (re-review N3). Should the group keep this caution, or follow CDC and allow short-acting prescription sleeping pills such as zolpidem 5 mg?
- **Reviewed date (all Task 12 protocols).** The content guide says "reviewed": "2026-11-16"; the sources were read on 2026-11-17, so the files carry 2026-11-17 (as Task 11 did).
- **Snakebite: how high to hold the bitten limb (snakebite) — Task 13.** Red Cross lay page: keep the injured area still and lower than the heart. WMS 2015 pit viper guideline (grade 2C): keep it at heart level; raising it may spread venom, lowering it may increase swelling. CDC/NIOSH: a neutral position of comfort. AHA 2024 7.4 gives no height. snakebite s5 uses Red Cross (highest authority that states one) and gives all three; all agree on not raising it. Should the group train "below the heart" or "at heart level"?
- **Pressure immobilization for coral snakes (snakebite) — Task 13.** AHA 2024 7.4 (Class 3: Harm, LOE C-LD): pressure immobilization bandaging is potentially harmful and not currently recommended in North America (because more than 95% of bites are pit vipers); the supportive text says it may have a role in neurotoxic snakebite. Red Cross lay page: never after a pit viper bite; for a coral snake bite with a prolonged transport, it may be considered by those trained in proper application, and gives the technique. WMS 2015 does not cover coral snakes. snakebite follows AHA (no pressure bandage for any snake) and states the Red Cross exception in s6. Does the group want a trained-only coral snake pressure-bandage step?
- **Walking out after a snakebite (snakebite) — Task 13.** AHA 2024: rest and minimize exertion only if this does not delay care; walking 10 minutes or more increased tracer spread; providers must balance the two. Red Cross: do not let the person walk unless absolutely necessary. snakebite s10/s11 asks "Will they have to walk to reach help?" and, if so, gives a how-to (walk slowly with rests, carry nothing, never alone, stop and wait for a carry or rescue if they become faint, confused, short of breath, weak or vomit) that is the group's judgement, not from a source. Is that acceptable, or should the group always wait for a carry-out when one is possible within some time limit?
- **Stinger removal method (stings) — Task 13.** AHA 2024 7.1 (Class 2a): remove the stinger as soon as possible by plucking or scraping (no difference in a volunteer study; speed matters, venom flows for up to 60 seconds). Red Cross lay: use a clean fingernail or tweezers to grasp and pull it out. MedlinePlus Insect bites and stings and CDC/NIOSH: scrape it off with a card, gauze or fingernail; never squeeze it or use tweezers. stings s6 follows AHA ("scrape or pluck, whichever is quickest") and states the others. Should the group teach one method?
- **Pain medicine after insect stings (stings) — Task 13.** AHA 2024 7.1 (Class 2b): over-the-counter acetaminophen and NSAIDs may be considered for local pain; (2a) oral antihistamines and topical steroids for itching. MedlinePlus: do not give stimulants, aspirin or other pain medicine unless prescribed. stings s11 follows AHA with the by-mouth exclusions in the warning. Acceptable?
- **Many stings, children, wasps and yellow jackets (stings) — Task 13, revised after review I5.** AHA 2024 7.1: death from massive envenomation can occur, generally from at least 20 stings by large hornets or hundreds of honeybee stings; no evacuation number and no figure for children. poison.org (Bee stings: is it an allergic reaction?): a person stung multiple times can develop headache, fever, nausea and dizziness. No opened source gives a care threshold. stings s7/s8 now evacuates at dozens of stings from bees, wasps, yellow jackets or hornets, or any child with several stings (group judgement; AHA's figures given only as context), and s12 evacuates anyone with headache, fever, nausea, dizziness, vomiting, weakness or confusion after several stings (poison.org lists the first four). After re-review N4 the child trigger names the insects (several bee, wasp, yellow jacket or hornet stings) and fire ant stings are not counted (group judgement; no source gives a fire ant number). Should the group set a lower trigger for children, is "dozens" (wasps and yellow jackets included) the right number, and should fire ant stings in a child have their own number?
- **Watch period after a sting (stings) — Task 13.** CDC/NIOSH: have someone stay with the stung person to see if an allergic reaction develops; no source opened gives a time. stings s12 says at least the next hour (group judgement). Acceptable?
- **Jellyfish included (stings) — Task 13.** AHA 2024 7.5 covers jellyfish stings in North America, so jellyfish are in the stings protocol (tentacles lifted off without bare skin or seawater rinse, hot non-scalding soak at least 20 minutes (Red Cross), 40 C / 104 F the most studied temperature (AHA), lidocaine). AHA makes no recommendation on vinegar and found it less effective than hot water; no opened source mentions fresh water. Should vinegar be mentioned at all?
- **Leftover tick mouthparts (tick) — Task 13.** CDC: if they will not come out easily with tweezers, leave them; the skin will push them out. Red Cross: see a healthcare provider if mouthparts remain embedded. tick s5 gives both. Off-grid, is a retained mouthpart a reason to seek care?
- **Bull's-eye rash (tick) — Task 13.** Red Cross Tick Bites marks a bull's-eye rash as needing immediate emergency medical treatment. CDC: see a doctor if a rash or fever develops within several days to weeks. tick lists the rash for evacuation, citing Red Cross. tick also evacuates urgently for fever or headache after a tick bite (group judgement based on CDC: Rocky Mountain spotted fever can become life-threatening quickly and its rash often comes 2 to 4 days after the fever). Are both the evacuation triggers the group wants?
- **Scorpion sting with only local pain (spider-scorpion) — Task 13.** Red Cross: have the person seek medical care immediately; treat all scorpion stings as medical emergencies. AHA 2024 7.6 (Class 1): seek care if pain spreads, is severe or uncontrolled by OTC medicine, an open wound develops or systemic symptoms appear; call EMS for systemic symptoms. AHA is the higher authority, but the protocol (s14) tells the user to get medical care for every scorpion sting, following Red Cross as the more cautious reading (labelled as a group judgement). Children always go urgently (AHA: breathing can be affected in children). Should adults with only local pain walk out normally and be watched instead?
- **Black widow and brown recluse (spider-scorpion) — Task 13.** Red Cross: keep the area elevated and as still as possible until EMS arrives (implying a 911 call for these bites). AHA 2024: call EMS only for body-wide symptoms; seek care for spreading or severe pain or an open wound. spider-scorpion s31 says keep it still and raised and get medical care; urgent evacuation only for body-wide signs (AHA). Acceptable?
- **Rabies urgency (animal-bite) — Task 13, revised after review I1.** CDC Clinical Screening for Rabies PEP: a patient should be immediately assessed for rabies-related care if they had direct contact with bats (unless a bite or scratch can be definitively ruled out), exposure to high-risk animals (typically bats, raccoons, skunks, foxes and mongoose), severe exposures (multiple bite wounds or the head and neck), or are a young child; most other exposures need medical attention quickly but not an immediate trip to the emergency room unless the wound needs urgent care. CDC PEP guidance and the Yellow Book give PEP regardless of the time since exposure. WMS 2014 (wound-care s3): within 24 to 48 hours. MedlinePlus: within 24 hours for any bite that breaks the skin. Red Cross: call 911 for a bite from an animal that could have rabies. animal-bite s11 now says "start evacuating now for rabies care" for CDC's immediate groups and "prompt care" for the rest. Should the app say "start evacuating now" for every possible rabies exposure, or only CDC's immediate groups as it does now?
- **Trapping a bat (animal-bite) — Task 13.** CDC Preventing Rabies from Bats: if safe, trap a bat that had contact with someone (leather gloves, box or jar, cardboard, tape) so it can be tested, and do not release it before talking to public health. animal-bite s12 gives this, "only if safe". Off-grid, testing may be days away. Keep this advice?
- **Drinks after a swallowed poison, and caustic swallows with no signal (poisoning) — Task 13, revised after review M3.** Red Cross Poison Exposure: do not give anything to eat or drink unless advised to. Poison Control (poison.org) instructs: if the product is burning, irritating or caustic, drink a small amount of water or milk immediately, provided the person is conscious, not having convulsions and able to swallow. MedlinePlus: nothing by mouth to an unconscious person. poisoning s10 follows Red Cross (higher authority) and quotes poison.org in its own direction. With no signal there is nobody to advise. Should poison.org's small drink of water or milk apply when Poison Control cannot be reached?
- **Poisoning with no signal (poisoning) — Task 13.** All sources rely on Poison Control or 911. poisoning s14 tells the user what to do without signal (follow the protocol, nothing by mouth, record what and when, do not wait for symptoms) and the evacuate list adds: with no signal, evacuate anyone who swallowed a medicine overdose, a caustic or burning substance, fuel, a pesticide, a wild mushroom or plant, or anything unknown, and anyone with any symptom. That list is the group's judgement. Is it right?
- **Rinsing chemicals off in the cold (poisoning) — Task 13.** AHA 2024 8.2 and poison.org: rinse skin for at least 15 minutes. poisoning s8 adds a labelled group judgement: in the cold, rinse in the warmest place you can and dry and warm them straight afterwards. Acceptable?
- **Poison Control number shown as text (poisoning, snakebite) — Task 13.** As the controller allowed, poisoning and snakebite show "Call Poison Control at 1-800-222-1222 if you have signal" as plain text; protocol text is rendered as escaped text, not a link. src/shell.html has no format-detection meta tag, so iOS Safari may turn the number into a tappable phone link (Android Chrome does not by default). The carbon-monoxide protocol still leaves the number out (Task 12 decision). Should shell.html get a format-detection meta tag, and should carbon-monoxide now show the number?
- **Which side to lie a poisoned person on (poisoning) — Task 13.** MedlinePlus Poisoning first aid: roll the person onto the left side. Red Cross and the Recovery position protocol: recovery position, either side. poisoning s21 sends to the Recovery position protocol and mentions MedlinePlus's left side. Does the side matter for the group?
- **Opioid overdose pointer (poisoning) — Task 13.** poisoning s15, s20 and s21 refer to the Opioid overdose protocol, which is written in the next batch (id overdose). Until it exists, a user following the pointer will not find it. s20 also says to give naloxone if available (AHA 2024 8.1). Should Task 14 check these pointers once it lands?
- **Antihistamine tablets for poison ivy (poison-plants) — Task 13.** AHA 2024 7.3 (Class 2b): benefit of OTC oral antihistamines uncertain; experts suggest them mainly for sleep. CDC/NIOSH: diphenhydramine may reduce itching but causes drowsiness. MedlinePlus: may help if creams and baths do not. poison-plants s7 offers them, with a group caution that a drowsy person should not navigate, drive or stand watch. Acceptable?
- **Red Cross First Aid Guidelines database blocked (all Task 13) — Task 13.** firstaidguide.wpenginepowered.com (ARC-DB, used in Tasks 10 and 11) now refuses automated fetching (robots disallowed), so the Red Cross Scientific Advisory Council answers for snakebite, stings, ticks, spiders, bites and poisoning could not be read. Task 13 uses the Red Cross lay pages and AHA/Red Cross 2024 instead. Should someone read the ARC-DB pages for these topics by hand and compare?
- **Going into fumes to rescue (poisoning s1, carbon-monoxide s1) — Task 13 review I2.** Red Cross Poison Exposure: remove the person from the source of the poison, if possible, without endangering yourself (carbon monoxide: remove the person if it is safe to do so). MedlinePlus Poisoning first aid: never attempt a rescue without notifying others first; if safe, take several deep breaths, hold your breath going in, hold a wet cloth over the nose and mouth. poisoning s1 and carbon-monoxide s1 now allow only a quick reach into a tent, shelter or vehicle on a held breath, tell others first, and forbid entering an enclosed space full of gas (pit, tank, well, cave); that limit is a group judgement. Should the app allow the quick breath-hold reach but forbid entering enclosed spaces?
- **Chemical in the eye: poisoning vs Eye injury — Task 13 review I3.** AHA 2024 8.3 (Class 1): copious tap water for 15 minutes. poison.org: at least 15 to 20 minutes; urgent eye exam if symptoms last an hour after rinsing started; straight to an emergency room for severe symptoms. MedlinePlus (via Eye injury s3): any chemical in the eye needs emergency care; keep flushing if it still hurts. Red Cross: keep flushing until EMS takes over. poisoning s6 now starts the rinse and sends the user to the Eye injury protocol for time and evacuation, and its evacuate list follows Eye injury (every chemical eye exposure gets medical care). Should every chemical eye exposure be evacuated, as the Eye injury protocol says?
- **Rabies from small rodents and rabbits, and pets that can be watched (animal-bite) — Task 13 review.** animal-bite s10 treats any wild animal acting strangely, and any stray or unvaccinated dog or cat, as a possible rabies exposure. CDC Yellow Book: observation periods exist only for dogs, cats and ferrets (an animal healthy 10 days after the exposure was not infectious); rodent bites are controversial and need individual assessment. CDC Clinical Screening does not address rodents or rabbits. Should a bite from a squirrel, chipmunk, mouse or rabbit, or from a known pet that can be watched for 10 days, still trigger evacuation for rabies shots?
- **Large chemical skin exposure (poisoning) — Task 13 review I4.** AHA 2024 8.2 (Class 1): EMS should be called when a person with chemical exposure has respiratory symptoms, systemic symptoms, or large chemical exposures. poisoning s4b, s8 and the evacuate list now say get help now for a large area of skin soaked with chemical, even without burns. No source defines "large". What size of soaked area should count for the group?
- **Snakebite on the face, neck or body (snakebite) — Task 13 review M8.** None of the opened sources gives separate advice. snakebite s5 detail and the evacuate list now say (group judgement) evacuate urgently and watch breathing because face or neck swelling may threaten the airway. Acceptable?
- **Walking out with chest pain (chest-pain) — Task 14.** AHA 2024 6.4: EMS transport to the closest emergency department; about 1 in 300 people taken by private vehicle arrested on the way. Red Cross: stop activity and rest; never drive the person yourself. No source covers a place EMS cannot reach. chest-pain s9 (group judgement): wait for a rescue or carry if one can come; walking out is the last option, slowly, carrying nothing, stopping if symptoms return. Should the group set a rule (for example, always wait for a rescue if one can arrive within some number of hours)?
- **Aspirin: encourage, or only when told (chest-pain) — Task 14.** AHA 2024 6.4 (Class 2b): providers may encourage alert adults with non-traumatic chest pain to chew and swallow 162 to 325 mg unless allergic or told not to; if uncertain, wait. MedlinePlus Heart attack first aid: only if instructed by a physician or 911 operator, not allergic, and no condition that makes aspirin dangerous. Used AHA (higher authority). Off-grid there is usually no operator to ask. Is the AHA approach acceptable for the group?
- **Nitroglycerin exclusions (chest-pain) — Task 14.** Red Cross and MedlinePlus say only to help with the person's own prescribed nitroglycerin; neither gives a dose or exclusions. The protocol adds only "fully alert and able to swallow". Should a medical adviser give the group any further exclusions to carry?
- **Sugar and nothing by mouth with stroke signs (stroke, low-blood-sugar) — Task 14, revised after review I4.** No opened source (AHA 2024 6.2, Red Cross Stroke, MedlinePlus Stroke, American Stroke Association) states a nothing-by-mouth rule for stroke; Red Cross lists drooling and trouble swallowing as stroke signs, and the American Stroke Association advises against aspirin. AHA 2024 6.2 names low blood sugar as a common stroke mimic and allows a glucose check only if it does not delay EMS; AHA 6.7 (Class 1) gives oral glucose to anyone awake and able to swallow; Red Cross Diabetic Emergencies says that if you cannot tell whether sugar is low or high, it is good to give some sugar. Both protocols now use one rule: someone with stroke signs who has diabetes treated with insulin or pills, or a measured reading below 70 mg/dL, and who is fully awake and swallows safely, gets at least 20 g of sugar and keeps evacuating as a stroke; anyone not fully awake or unable to swallow gets nothing by mouth (AHA harm). Other food, drink and medicine are withheld as a group judgement. Should a person with stroke signs and no diabetes ever get sugar without a meter, and is withholding other drinks right on a long evacuation?
- **AHA mmol/L figure (stroke, low-blood-sugar) — Task 14.** AHA 2024 6.7 prints hypoglycaemia as "<70 or <2.8 mmol/L"; 70 mg/dL is 3.9 mmol/L, which AHA itself gives as the lower edge of normal. The protocols give mg/dL only. Do you want mmol/L shown, and if so which figure?
- **Sugar amount and recheck time (low-blood-sugar) — Task 14.** AHA 2024 6.7: at least 20 g of glucose; activate EMS if no improvement within 10 minutes. CDC (15-15 rule): 15 g, wait 15 minutes, repeat while below 70 mg/dL. Used AHA (20 g, 10 minute timer). AHA does not say whether to repeat; s9 gives a second 20 g while help is on the way, drawn from CDC's repeat. Acceptable?
- **Seizure emergency list (seizure) — Task 14.** AHA 2024 6.6 lists specific triggers (first seizure, over 5 minutes, repeated without recovery, in water, injury, breathing trouble or choking, infant under 6 months, pregnancy, not back to normal in 5 to 10 minutes). Red Cross also marks loss of consciousness and whole-body convulsions as needing immediate care, which would include almost every tonic-clonic seizure in someone with known epilepsy. Used AHA for the question (Red Cross given in the detail). Should a typical short seizure in a member with known epilepsy, who recovers, mean evacuation off-grid?
- **Seizure in water technique (seizure) — Task 14, updated after review M5.** AHA, CDC and Red Cross all call a seizure in water an emergency but give no technique. Michigan Medicine, Seizure First Aid and Safety (revised 11/2019, outside the top tier): support the person with the head above water, tilted back if possible, and send them to an emergency department whether or not they seem to fully recover. s30 now follows that; bringing them out once safe for the rescuer is a group judgement. Is that what the group wants to train?
- **Asthma: number of puffs (asthma) — Task 14.** AHA 2024 6.1: help the person use their own bronchodilator as needed, spacer preferred; no number of puffs. Red Cross: use as directed by their healthcare provider; second dose if signs remain 10 to 15 minutes after the first. No opened source gives a puff count or doses beyond the second. Should members with asthma record their own action plan doses in the app?
- **Improvised spacer (asthma) — Task 14.** AHA 2024 6.1 (Class 2a): an improvised spacer from a 500 mL drink bottle or 150 mL paper cup is reasonable, but AHA gives no method. The protocol mentions it without a how-to. Do you want a sourced construction method added later?
- **Evacuation after a mild asthma attack (asthma) — Task 14.** Red Cross marks wheezing, coughing and trouble breathing as needing immediate emergency care, i.e. almost every attack. asthma s11 and the evacuate list leave the decision after a mild attack that fully settles with one dose to the group (judgement). Should any attack mean ending the trip?
- **Watching after naloxone (overdose) — Task 14.** CDC Lifesaving Naloxone: stay until help arrives or for at least four hours. NIDA: monitor for another 2 hours after the last dose. AHA 2025 21.11: observe in a health care setting until the risk is low (ideal time unknown; longer for long-acting opioids). Used CDC's 4 hours (timer). Is 4 hours right for the group, given evacuation may take longer?
- **Naloxone re-dose timer (overdose) — Task 14.** MedlinePlus: repeat every 2 to 3 minutes in alternate nostrils with a new device. The timer is set to 2 minutes, the earliest point. Acceptable?
- **Rescue breaths for slow breathing with a pulse (overdose) — Task 14.** AHA 2025 21.11 (Class 1) gives breaths to people with respiratory depression and a definite pulse, but only for trained rescuers; lay rescuers do CPR with breaths if not breathing normally. The protocol tells trained users to give breaths and everyone else to start CPR if breathing stops being normal. Is anyone in the group trained to give rescue breaths with a pulse check?
- **Raising the legs after a faint (fainting) — Task 14.** AHA 2024 5.2 (Class 2b): raising the feet 6 to 12 inches may be reasonable without trauma (e.g. simple fainting). MedlinePlus: raise the feet about 12 inches. Red Cross Fainting: does not mention leg raising (head level with the heart). Used AHA. Should the group keep the leg raise?
- **Fainting warning signs (fainting) — Task 14, updated after review M8.** MedlinePlus lists over 50, pregnancy and diabetes among reasons to call 911 for a person who fainted; the protocol now applies that list only to people who actually passed out, not to someone who only felt faint. With I3 every actual faint is evacuated anyway; the list decides urgency. Should the group treat these as urgent off-grid?
- **Anti-diarrhoeal medicine (vomiting-diarrhea) — Task 14.** AHA and Red Cross first aid guidelines do not cover it, and the content guide allows only medicines those guidelines give lay responders. MedlinePlus Diarrhea: OTC anti-diarrhoeals can be used unless there is fever or bloody diarrhoea. CDC Yellow Book (for clinicians advising travellers): loperamide for mild to moderate diarrhoea, not alone with blood or fever; bismuth subsalicylate exclusions. The protocol gives this as information in a detail with no dose, not as a step. Should loperamide become a step (with the CDC exclusions) for the group's kit?
- **Which vomiting and diarrhoea triggers (vomiting-diarrhea) — Task 14, revised after review I5.** Red Cross Dehydration (higher tier) marks as needing immediate emergency care: unable to keep fluids down, dizziness, severely decreased or no urination, no tears, vomiting or diarrhoea for more than a few days, sunken eyes or cheeks, tented skin, sweet breath, bloody or black stool, sharp or persistent belly pain, shock; it has no count of vomits. MedlinePlus Viral gastroenteritis (2026): contact a provider if diarrhoea lasts more than several days or dehydration occurs, with a sign list; no count of vomits. MedlinePlus Nausea and vomiting: care right away for vomiting over 24 hours, no fluids kept down for 12 hours, no urine for 8 hours, 3 or more vomits in a day, headache or stiff neck. s9 now leads with the Red Cross list plus MedlinePlus's headache or stiff neck; the MedlinePlus figures are information in the detail, not the trigger. Is that the trigger set the group wants?
- **Sips with severe belly pain or blood in the vomit (vomiting-diarrhea) — Task 14, extended after review M11.** MedlinePlus Abdominal pain: sips of water or clear fluids for mild pain; no medicine without a provider. MedlinePlus Nausea and vomiting: blood or coffee-ground material in the vomit means 911 or an emergency room. The Dehydration protocol withholds drinks when an injury may need surgery. No source covers sips for possible appendicitis or for vomiting blood on a long evacuation. s24 leaves sips with severe belly pain to the group; s25 now gives nothing by mouth to someone vomiting blood or coffee-ground material (group judgement). Should either get small sips during a multi-hour evacuation?
- **Every actual faint means medical care (fainting) — Task 14 review I3.** AHA 2024 6.8 (Class 2b): activate EMS if syncope occurs; Red Cross Fainting asterisks loss of consciousness (comes to in about a minute) as needing immediate emergency treatment. The protocol now sends everyone who actually passed out to medical care (urgently if a MedlinePlus warning sign is present). Off-grid that means evacuating, for example, a young, uninjured member after a typical faint on standing who recovers fully. Does the group want a narrower rule for such cases?
- **Feeling faint without passing out (fainting) — Task 14 review.** AHA 2024 6.8: activate EMS only if there is no improvement within 1 to 2 minutes, a faint occurs, or symptoms worsen or recur. The Red Cross Fainting page also asterisks pale skin or sweating, and weakness or dizziness, as needing immediate emergency treatment, which would cover almost every faint feeling. The protocol follows AHA (higher authority). Is that acceptable?
- **Evacuating during the naloxone watch (overdose) — Task 14 review I1.** Red Cross: immediate medical care even after naloxone; AHA 2025: observe in a health care setting; SAMHSA 2013: to medical care as quickly as possible even if they revive and seem better. CDC: stay with them until help arrives or for at least four hours; SAMHSA: monitor at least 4 hours from the last dose. The protocol now starts the evacuation at once (or waits for a rescue already called) and does the 4-hour watch on the move, timed from the last dose. Is that what the group wants?
- **Revived person who wants to leave (overdose) — Task 14 review I2.** SAMHSA 2013: withdrawal after naloxone can feel unpleasant; some people become agitated or combative and need help to stay calm; comfort them; get them to medical care even if they seem better. No opened source covers someone who refuses care or wants to leave off-grid. The protocol says (group judgement): do not let them go off alone; if they will not wait or come, someone stays with them carrying naloxone; do not restrain them or put yourself at risk. What should the group do if a revived person insists on leaving or on using again?
- **Sports drinks for vomiting and diarrhoea (vomiting-diarrhea, dehydration) — Task 14 review.** MedlinePlus Viral gastroenteritis (000252, reviewed 2026-04-27), read word for word: "Older children and adults can drink sports beverages such as Gatorade, but these should be avoided. Instead, use the electrolyte and fluid replacement solutions..."; do not use fruit juice, sodas or cola, fruit-flavoured gelatin or broth; drink 2 to 4 ounces (60 to 120 mL) every 30 to 60 minutes. Red Cross Dehydration lists sports drinks with water and oral rehydration solutions; dehydration.json (AHA 2024 7.10, written for exertional dehydration) prefers a 4 to 9% carbohydrate-electrolyte drink to water, and gives packaged oral rehydration salts as best for heavy diarrhoea (CDC). vomiting-diarrhea s7 now lists oral rehydration solution first and gives the MedlinePlus wording in the detail; dehydration.json was not edited. Should the Dehydration protocol say that for vomiting and diarrhoea (as opposed to sweating) oral rehydration solution is preferred over sports drinks?
- **Two fever thresholds (vomiting-diarrhea, dehydration) — Task 14 review M15.** vomiting-diarrhea uses 101°F (38.3°C) with diarrhoea (MedlinePlus Diarrhea); dehydration.json uses over 102°F (38.9°C) (MedlinePlus Dehydration). Both are sourced, but a user may see both. Should the app use one number, and which?
- **Aspirin age limit (chest-pain) — Task 14 review I6.** AHA 2024 6.4 limits aspirin to "alert adults"; the protocol now excludes anyone under 18 (the app's reading of "adult"; AHA gives no age). Is 18 the right cut-off for the group?
- **Tourniquet time on long evacuations — Task 15 update (bleeding, tourniquet-conversion).** The Tourniquet reassessment and conversion protocol (advanced) now gives the TCCC 2026 rules: convert as soon as possible when the casualty is not in shock, the wound can be watched closely and it is not an amputation; every effort within 2 hours; All Service Member and Combat Lifesaver level personnel not beyond 2 hours unless a combat medic, paramedic or other advanced medical person directs it; never remove after 6 hours without close monitoring and laboratory capability; without medical oversight, leave it on. The Wilderness Medical Society 2014 wound guideline says the opposite for the 2-to-6-hour window: a tourniquet left on longer than 2 hours should stay in place until definitive medical evaluation. The protocol follows TCCC 2026. Which group members will train to convert, and should the group follow TCCC or WMS after 2 hours?
- **Who may convert a tourniquet (tourniquet-conversion) — Task 15.** The TCCC Skill Sets by Responder Level matrix (25 Jan 2024) lists tourniquet conversion from Combat Lifesaver level; the TCCC 2026 Summary of Changes says All Service Members and Combat Lifesavers are now expected to reposition and convert. The summary line states both. Should the group treat conversion as an all-member skill or a Combat Lifesaver-level one?
- **NPA: which TCCC edition's indication (npa) — Task 15.** TCCC 2026 (5e, unchanged from Jan 2024) mentions the NPA only with bag-valve-mask ventilation for impaired ventilation and hypoxia that cannot be corrected, and puts an unconscious breathing casualty in the recovery position. The 2020 Combat Lifesaver course (speaker notes and skill instructions) inserted an NPA in any casualty breathing but unconscious or semi-conscious without obstruction. The protocol lists the 2026 indication first and the 2020 one second. Without a bag-valve-mask, should the group use an NPA for an unconscious breathing casualty, or rely on the recovery position as TCCC now does?
- **NPA facial injury (npa) — Task 15, reworded after review A I8.** Current TCCC (2026 guideline; CMC NPA card SC-29, 2025; CMC skill instructions 2021) lists no NPA contraindication besides never forcing the tube. The 2020 CLS course adds clear fluid from the nose or ears. Only StatPearls (outside the top tier) rules out facial trauma or midface disruption (absolute) and polyps, recent nose surgery, bleeding disorders and blood thinners (relative). The app follows StatPearls, the strictest, and attributes it as "(StatPearls; current TCCC lists none)". Is that what the group wants?
- **Needle decompression: which site first (needle-decompression) — Task 15.** TCCC 2026 lists the 5th intercostal space, anterior axillary line first, and says either may be used; the CPP job aid (2023) lists the 2nd intercostal space, mid-clavicular line first; both skill sheets say use whichever is more accessible. The 2020 Combat Lifesaver speaker notes gave a different trigger ("the sole criterion... is a chest wound with increasing respiratory difficulty"); the protocol uses the 2026 trigger (torso trauma or blast plus a listed sign). How to find the 5th or 2nd rib space by counting is not described in words in any source read and is left to hands-on training. Which site should the group train first?
- **Cric failure plan and capnography (cricothyrotomy) — Task 15, updated after review A I2.** No TCCC document read (CMC skill instructions and checklist 2021, CMC cards 2023, TCCC 2026) gives a procedure for a failed cric, and TCCC 2026 confirms placement with continuous end-tidal CO2, which the group is unlikely to carry; the app uses the skill-sheet checks (misting, breath sounds on both sides, equal chest rise). cricothyrotomy s25 now reads, labelled as a group judgement: "Group judgement: no misting, chest rise or breath sounds on either side: the tube is not in the windpipe; do not bag through it, control the opening, evacuate now. Otherwise: see detail.", with the detail adding "if the face allows, ventilate with the bag-valve-mask over the mouth and nose". The child cut-off (12 years) is from StatPearls' relative range "below 5 to 12 years". The wording does not say what to do with the misplaced tube itself (leave it in or remove it); no source covers that either. Two more group judgements were added in round 3: a casualty with misting or chest rise but unequal breath sounds after rechecking has a working tube and is ventilated through it as needed while evacuating now (s25 detail); and the tube may be tried once more after a failed attempt (s13, citing the TCCC standard of an airway in 4 minutes), with a second failure going to the failure step (at most two tube attempts). Will a medical adviser review and approve this failure wording, the two-attempt limit and the unequal-sounds rule, say what to do with the tube and how to control an opening with nothing in it, and confirm the child rule, or give others?
- **Hemostatic pressure time (hemostatic-packing, junctional-bleeding, tourniquet-conversion) — Task 15.** TCCC 2026 and every TCCC skill sheet: at least 3 minutes of direct pressure. WMS 2014 (1B): at least 5 minutes for hemostatic agents. The timers use 3 minutes (TCCC, higher on the authority list) and the detail says pressing longer does no harm. Which should the group train?
- **Pulling out packing (hemostatic-packing vs bleeding) — Task 15.** The Severe bleeding protocol (lay, plain gauze) says never pull out packing; press harder or add more. TCCC skill sheets say the same for plain gauze, but for a hemostatic dressing that fails: remove it and repack with a fresh one (TCCC 2026: each dressing works differently). The advanced protocol follows TCCC for hemostatic dressings only. Is that split acceptable?
- **Groin landmark wording (junctional-bleeding) — Task 15.** CLS skill instructions 2020: the inguinal gutter is halfway between the bone above the genitals and the top of the thigh. CMC skill card 2021: halfway between the pubic bone and the anterior superior iliac spine. The SAM, CRoC and JETT sheets press over the femoral pulse just below the inguinal ligament (or just below the midpoint of the line from the anterior superior iliac spine to the pubic tubercle). The step gives both wordings. No source read gives a time limit for a junctional tourniquet; the protocol says treat it like a tourniquet and record the time. Does the group want a limit from a medical adviser?
- **Pelvic binder duration (pelvic-binder) — Task 15, replaced after review B I5.** JTS Pelvic Fracture Care CPG 34 (17 Feb 2026): once a binder has been on more than 6 hours, check the skin under it at least every 12 hours; binders or sheets should not be left on more than 24 hours if possible; the guideline says nothing about loosening in the field. TCCC proposed change 1602 (2016): reassess and loosen at about 8 to 12 hours if stable. The app now states JTS 34 and tells the user not to loosen it. Which should the app state, and who decides on a multi-day evacuation?
- **Traction splint (removed) — Task 15, replaced after review B I11.** traction-splint.json was removed: TCCC's Skill Sets (25 Jan 2024) list "Traction Splinting of a Extremity Fracture" under "REMOVED in 2024"; TCCC 2026 does not mention it; JTS CPG 56 (2026) says only that traction splints may be used for femur fractures; no top-tier source gives the steps (StatPearls, a second source, does). The keywords traction splint, sager and hare now lead to Broken bone (fracture), which says traction splints are not covered in this app. Should it stay removed, or come back as a non-draft pointer entry with no technique?
- **Bites on the face (wound-closure) — Task 15.** WMS 2014 counts animal and human bites anywhere except the face as contaminated (implying face bites may be closed) and delays closure 4 days for rabies-prone wounds; BSA Wilderness First Aid leaves animal bites open. The protocol leaves every bite open. Should face bites ever be closed in the field?
- **Gaping wounds (wound-closure) — Task 15.** BSA Wilderness First Aid: wounds gaping more than half an inch should not be closed in the field but evacuated for closure by a physician. WMS 2014 gives no gape limit and says most wounds can be closed primarily (1B), with sutures or staples preferred for complex wounds. The protocol uses the BSA limit because this app does not describe sutures or staples. Acceptable?
- **Dislocation source (dislocation-reduction) — Task 15, updated after review B.** No Wilderness Medical Society clinical practice guideline on dislocations was found. The protocol now leads with the American Red Cross Wilderness and Remote First Aid Emergency Reference Guide (2014, pp. 37-39), which is above BSA in the authority order and describes the same techniques, then the BSA Wilderness First Aid guidelines (2017, with WMS input) and StatPearls. Review B also found Pringle et al. (Wilderness Environ Med 2023;34:410-419), a ski-patrol EMT series supporting anterior shoulder reduction by trained non-physicians (not opened by the author; no technique list). Is that source set acceptable to you?
- **Reduction with poor circulation (dislocation-reduction) — Task 15.** BSA: go fast for injuries that reduce circulation or sensation beyond the injury; it does not say whether to try a reduction first. StatPearls (hospital setting): poor circulation or nerve damage where prompt reduction is not possible without further injury is a reason not to reduce; avoid repeated attempts. The protocol allows one gentle attempt by a trained person while the evacuation starts (labelled group judgement). Is that what the group wants?
- **How to tell a dislocation is back (dislocation-reduction) — Task 15.** BSA lists the signs of a dislocation (pain, loss of normal range of movement, abnormal look) and says the shoulder slips and the kneecap often pops back, but gives no explicit success check. The protocol reads success as the joint looking like the other side again with movement returning. Should a medical adviser confirm the check the group uses?
- **TCCC training tiers read from the skill matrix (all Task 15) — Task 15.** The TCCC Skill Sets by Responder Level PDF (25 Jan 2024) text gives each row's X marks without column alignment; tiers were inferred from the number of marks (four marks: all levels; three: CLS and up; two: CM/HM and CP) and cross-checked against which course's skill sheets teach the skill (NPA, needle decompression and conversion in CLS sheets; cric, junctional devices in CMC sheets; pelvic binder in a CPP card, with CLS notes saying only to suspect it and notify medical personnel). Should someone confirm the tiers by looking at the PDF itself?
- **9-line line 9 and line 8 wording (Task 7 report builder) — Task 15.** The TCCC Combat Medic/Corpsman skill card #TCCC-CMC-SC-60 (2021), used for the app, gives line 9 wartime as NBC contamination with N nuclear, B biological, C chemical, and line 8 E as "Enemy Prisoner". A NATO 9-line table hosted on the same Deployed Medicine server (no issuer or date) gives line 9 wartime as chemical, biological, radiological and nuclear contamination (C, B, R, N) and line 8 E as "Enemy prisoner of war". Current US Army doctrine (ATP 4-02.2) was not opened. Line 6 in the app carries only the wartime security options; the peacetime line 6 (number and type of wound, injury or illness) has no field. Review B: CLS Module 19 Skill Instructions #TCCC-CLS-09-01 (25 Jan 2020) gives line 9 wartime as "CBRN Contamination" with N, B, C, line 8 E as "Enemy Prisoner (EPW)", line 1 "(8-digit grid coordinate)", and repeats the line 6 peacetime text under line 9 peacetime (apparently an error). The app's line 9 label now matches SC-60 ("Include this line only when applicable"). For an off-grid civilian group the peacetime variants (line 6 wound types, line 9 terrain) are the ones that apply, but line 6 offers only wartime security codes. Should line 9 add R (radiological), and should the form default to peacetime (a wartime/peacetime mode is being added separately)?
- **No second conversion after a rebleed (tourniquet-conversion) — Task 15 re-review B N1.** TCCC CLS Speaker Notes #TCCC-CLS-14-01 (17 Apr 2024): after a failed conversion "it is appropriate to try again within the next two hours", as long as it has not been more than six hours since the original tourniquet was applied. tourniquet-conversion s26y (a wound that bled again after conversion and is now controlled by the tourniquet) says do not try conversion again, labelled the app's caution, and states the TCCC allowance in its detail. Should the group follow TCCC's retry allowance instead?
- **Cric requires a bag-valve-mask before starting (cricothyrotomy) — Task 15 review A.** The app does not start a cric without a bag-valve-mask. TCCC ventilates only if breathing is below 8 or above 30, or oxygen below 90%, so a casualty breathing on their own may not need one. Should the group allow a cric without a bag?
- **Junctional tourniquet timing (junctional-bleeding) — Task 15 review A.** TCCC 2026 applies a junctional tourniquet immediately once ready, with packing and pressure only while it is readied; the CMC skill instructions (2021) apply it after packing (wound packing steps 1 to 5), before the 3-minute hold. The protocol now asks about the groin right after packing and applies the device as soon as it is ready, a helper readying it during packing. Should the group train it that way?
- **Pulseless limb after a pressure bandage (hemostatic-packing) — Task 15 review A.** The TCCC skill says only loosen and reapply. hemostatic-packing s16x (group judgement): if the limb stays cool or pulseless after one rewrap, keep the bandage firm enough to control bleeding and evacuate urgently. Is that acceptable, or should the app say something else?
- **Order of decompression in traumatic arrest (needle-decompression) — Task 15 review A.** TCCC 2026 says decompress both sides and gives no order. The app no longer says right first; it says one side, then the other. Does the group want a fixed order for training?
- **Repositioning an old tourniquet (tourniquet-conversion) — Task 15 review B I4.** TCCC 2026 and the 2026 reposition skill (CMC card SC-37, CPP skill instructions 09-03) set no time limit on repositioning a high-and-tight or over-clothing tourniquet; the 2026 summary names reperfusion injury as a concern, and the 6-hour rule covers removal. The app now asks the 6-hour question before repositioning (conservative; app's caution): over 6 hours, leave it, do not reposition or convert, evacuate urgently. Should the app offer repositioning after 6 hours, or after 2 hours to non-medics?
- **Tourniquet removal for lay wilderness responders (tourniquet-conversion, bleeding) — Task 15 review B.** American Red Cross Wilderness and Remote First Aid (2014): keep the tourniquet tight and in place until the person reaches advanced medical care; do not remove it unless a health care provider advises it. TCCC 2026 lets trained ASM/CLS convert within 2 hours. WMS 2014: keep it on after 2 hours. Should conversion be limited to members with formal TCCC training?
- **Pelvic binder indications (pelvic-binder) — Task 15 review B.** TCCC 2026: severe blunt or blast mechanism plus one of five signs. JTS CPG 56 (2026): "severe lower extremity/pelvic injury". JTS CPG 34 (2026): IED blast, crush and high-velocity penetrating wounds with low blood pressure as high risk, and signs such as a leg length difference or perineal bruising. Should a low-energy fall with clear pelvic signs get a binder?
- **Lay pelvic wrap (pelvic-binder) — Task 15 review B I6.** Red Cross Wilderness and Remote First Aid (2014, p. 36) and BSA tell lay responders to tie a wide sheet or blanket around the pelvis for support; TCCC reserves the pelvic compression device for medics. pelvic-binder s30 (untrained or no material) now says: no tight binder, but a wide sheet or blanket tied snugly around the hips. Should untrained users be told to wrap?
- **Stimson weight (dislocation-reduction) — Task 15 review B.** Red Cross and BSA: about 5 to 10 pounds. StatPearls Shoulder Dislocations Overview (2023): 5 to 15 pounds, reduction usually within 30 minutes. The app uses 5 to 10. Which range should the group train?
- **Wound closure time window (wound-closure) — Task 15 review B.** WMS 2014: 6 hours (10 for face and scalp) in the 1C recommendation; Table 3 treats any wound presenting after 8 to 12 hours (24 on the face) as contaminated. The app uses 6 and 10. Keep the stricter limit?
- **Armpit and groin wounds (wound-closure) — Task 15 review B I7.** WMS 2014 Table 3 says to consider wounds in the axilla or groin dirty; its table still closes dirty wounds primarily (only contaminated wounds get delayed closure). The app now leaves armpit and groin wounds open as a caution. Is that stricter rule what the group wants?
- **Reduction with poor circulation — update (dislocation-reduction) — Task 15 review B I10.** The flow now enforces the single attempt: with poor circulation beyond the joint, a shoulder gets only the quick self-reduction if it came out minutes ago (no 20 to 30 minute Stimson), a kneecap, finger or toe gets one attempt, any failure goes to splint and evacuate rapidly, and still-poor circulation after a successful reduction goes to rapid evacuation (Red Cross: evacuate rapidly for decreased circulation, sensation or movement). Is one attempt right for the group?

## bleeding
- Sources read: AHA-FA 2024 section 9.1; ARC-DB Wounds and Bleeding (direct pressure ILCOR 2020/2023 update; hemostatic dressings 2025 update; tourniquets 2021; improvised tourniquets 2025 update; pediatric tourniquets 2022 update; sequence of interventions 2024/2025); STB lay presentation v2 (2019); TCCC 2026 Massive Hemorrhage and Tourniquet Reassessment.
- Numbers checked:
  - 2 to 3 inches above the wound, not over a joint — tourniquet placement — ARC-DB Tourniquets "Insights"; STB "Apply 2 to 3 inches above wound, do not place over elbow or knee"; TCCC 3a "2-3 inches above the bleeding site". Checked twice.
  - Second tourniquet side by side with the first — TCCC 3a and 6g; ARC-DB tourniquet insights (first ineffective, bleeding resumes, large or muscular limb). Checked twice.
