  - About 2 years, limb 13 cm (about 5 inches) — pediatric tourniquet threshold — ARC-DB Pediatric Tourniquets guidelines; AHA-FA "children as young as 2 years". Checked twice.
  - At least 2 inches wide — improvised tourniquet — AHA-FA 9.1 (Class 2a). ARC-DB says 1.5 inches (see open questions). Checked twice.
  - Half a can of soda, 6 ounces — blood-loss guide — ARC-DB Identification of life-threatening bleeding (citing the Stop the Bleed Education Consortium, consensus-based). Checked once on drafting and again in the saved page text.
- Review fixes (2026-11-17): s4 "no" now goes to s12 (neck/armpit/groin or deep limb wound with no tourniquet → packing s8), then s13 (chest → s14: firm pressure without sealing, use Open chest wound, end; otherwise s15: firm direct pressure, end). Re-read AHA-FA 9.1/9.2 and ARC-DB sequence of interventions (saved page text). s8 action restricted to neck, armpit, groin or deep limb wound.
- Round 2 (2026-11-17): s13 "no" now goes to s16 "Are organs showing through the wound?" yes → s17 (no pushing back, no pressure, loose moist dressing, plastic wrap or foil over it, on the back with knees bent unless painful, shock care, end); no → s15 (pressure, with a `warn` about organs). Source: ARC-LAY Abdominal injury, https://www.redcross.org/take-a-class/resources/learn-first-aid/abdominal-injury (read 2026-11-17, "General Care: Open Abdominal Injury"). s14 detail reworded to the AHA-FA 9.2 point that any dressing over a chest opening can trap air. The s15 sentence on the Red Cross algorithm listing packing for head, neck and trunk wounds "if trained" is kept: the saved ARC-DB text (Sequence of interventions, Insights) reads "Direct pressure, with use of a hemostatic dressing, if available, and wound packing, if trained, are interventions for life-threatening bleeding from the head, neck and trunk."
- Round 3 (2026-11-17): s3 action text now excludes organs showing through a belly wound and carries a `warn` with the Red Cross care; s11 (not life-threatening path) carries the same `warn` and the Red Cross evisceration care in brief in its detail, because that path has no later organs question; s17 action shortened (help sentence moved to detail). Re-read ARC-LAY Abdominal injury, General Care: Open Abdominal Injury and positioning.
- Notes: Hemostatic gauze (ARC-DB/TCCC: at least 3 minutes of pressure) and tourniquet conversion (TCCC 2026: within 2 hours, not by ASM/CLS beyond 2 hours without direction) are deliberately not detailed; both point to "see Advanced procedures" per the task. Pressure points, elevation and cryotherapy are in the don'ts per AHA-FA/ARC-DB. Direct pressure has no timer: sources say hold until help arrives, not a fixed duration. Tourniquet on skin (TCCC) vs over clothes allowed (STB) both stated.

## cpr-adult
- Sources read: AHA-ABLS 2025 (cpr.heart.org page and ahajournals full text); AHA-SPEC 2025 drowning and opioid sections; AHA-HL 2025; ARC-LAY CPR steps and AED steps.
- Numbers checked:
  - At least 2 inches (5 cm), not more than 2.4 inches (6 cm) — compression depth — AHA-ABLS (Class 1). Checked twice.
  - 100 to 120 per minute — rate — AHA-ABLS (Class 2a). Checked twice.
  - 30:2 — single and two lay rescuers, coach ratio — AHA-ABLS (Class 2a). Checked twice.
  - About 1 second per breath, visible chest rise — AHA-ABLS (Class 2b / 2a). Checked twice.
  - Swap about every 2 minutes (about 5 cycles) — AHA-ABLS (Class 2a). Checked twice.
  - Lower half of the sternum — hand position — AHA-ABLS (Class 1). Checked twice.
  - Torso at about the rescuer's knee level — AHA-ABLS (Class 1). Checked twice.
- Notes: Compression-only for untrained or unwilling rescuers (AHA-ABLS, Class 1 for compressions; dispatcher hands-only for adults in AHA-HL). Drowning: CPR with breaths (Class 1); trained may start with breaths (2b); compression-only reasonable for adults if unable/unwilling (2a); CPR with breaths before AED (Class 1) — AHA-SPEC. Naloxone during arrest may be reasonable if it does not interfere with CPR (AHA-HL). Bra adjust rather than remove (2b). Naloxone wording re-verified 2026-11-17 in AHA-HL, "Toxicology: Opioid Overdose": opioid antagonist "may be reasonable" for lay and trained rescuers in cardiac arrest "provided that ... administration does not interfere with the delivery of standard resuscitation", including high-quality CPR with breaths. Lone-rescuer text re-read in AHA-ABLS (Class 1 B-NR activate first; Class 1 C-LD with phone, near-simultaneous). Round 2: supporting text "local circumstances will determine decisions about delaying CPR...; however, the importance of timely CPR must be emphasized" — both halves now in s2. Pad positions from ARC-LAY AED steps and AHA-ABLS (anterolateral or anteroposterior).

## cpr-child
- Sources read: AHA-PBLS 2025 (cpr.heart.org page and ahajournals full text); AHA-SPEC 2025 drowning; ARC-LAY child and baby CPR.
- Numbers checked:
  - At least one third of chest depth, about 2 inches (5 cm) — AHA-PBLS (Class 2a). Checked twice.
  - 100 to 120 per minute — AHA-PBLS (Class 2a). Checked twice.
  - 30:2 single rescuer (coach), 15:2 two rescuers — AHA-PBLS (Class 2a). Checked twice.
  - Pauses under 10 seconds — AHA-PBLS (Class 1). Checked twice.
  - Under 8 years: pediatric attenuator/pads — AHA-PBLS (Class 1). Checked twice.
  - 1 breath every 2 to 3 seconds — breathing inadequate with a pulse (trained rescuers) — AHA-PBLS (Class 2a). Checked twice.
  - About 1 year to puberty — age range — AHA-PBLS. Checked twice.
  - About 1 second per breath — ARC-LAY child and baby CPR. Checked once on drafting (lay page).
- Lone rescuer re-checked 2026-11-17 in the AHA 2025 pediatric single-rescuer algorithm long-description PDF (Box 5 witnessed collapse, 5a activate and AED, 7 "After 2 minutes, if still alone, activate the emergency response system").
- Notes: Conventional CPR with breaths should be given to children (Class 1); compression-only if unable or unwilling (2a). One- or two-hand technique (2b). Head tilt-chin lift (Class 1); jaw thrust if c-spine injury suspected, head tilt-chin lift if jaw thrust fails. Blind finger sweeps Class 3: Harm.

## cpr-infant
- Sources read: AHA-PBLS 2025; AHA-HL 2025 (infant technique change); AHA-SPEC drowning; ARC-LAY child and baby CPR.
- Numbers checked:
  - At least one third of chest depth, about 1.5 inches (4 cm) — AHA-PBLS (Class 2a); ARC-LAY "about 1 1/2 inches". Checked twice.
  - 100 to 120 per minute — AHA-PBLS. Checked twice.
  - 30:2 single rescuer (coach), 15:2 two rescuers — AHA-PBLS. Checked twice.
  - Under 10-second pauses — AHA-PBLS. Checked twice.
  - Younger than about 1 year — AHA-PBLS. Checked twice.
  - About 2 minutes (5 sets of 30:2) before a lone rescuer leaves — ARC-LAY child and baby CPR; confirmed 2026-11-17 in the AHA 2025 pediatric single-rescuer algorithm long-description PDF, Box 7. Checked twice.
  - 1 breath every 2 to 3 seconds — AHA-PBLS (with a pulse, trained). Checked twice.
- Notes: Positioning a baby who starts breathing (review issue 12): no AHA or Red Cross source found that describes it (only St John Ambulance UK, not in the authority list). Left unchanged: the step says to stay and watch breathing.
- Notes: Two-thumb encircling or heel of one hand (Class 1); two-finger technique eliminated (AHA-PBLS, AHA-HL). Thumbs "just below the nipple line" from ARC-LAY; "lower half of the sternum, avoiding the xiphoid" from AHA-PBLS Table 7. Infant airway to neutral position from ARC-LAY.

## choking
- Sources read: AHA-ABLS 2025 FBAO (recommendations and supporting text in ahajournals full text); AHA-PBLS 2025 FBAO (children); AHA-HL 2025; ARC-LAY adult and child choking.
- Numbers checked:
  - 5 back blows then 5 abdominal thrusts, repeated — AHA-ABLS (Class 1, B-NR); AHA-PBLS for children (Class 1); ARC-LAY. Checked twice.
  - 5 chest thrusts instead if cannot encircle or late pregnancy — AHA-ABLS (Class 1, C-EO). Checked twice.
  - Fist thumb side just above the navel (find navel with two fingers, fist above them), pull inward and upward 5 times — ARC-LAY adult and child choking, re-read 2026-11-17. "Well below the xiphoid" removed (could not re-open the AHA full text).
  - Back blows: side and slightly behind, one arm diagonally across the chest, bent forward until the upper body is parallel to the ground, 5 separate blows with the heel of the hand — ARC-LAY, re-read 2026-11-17; AHA-ABLS "standing to the side and slightly behind... leaning forward".
  - Chest thrusts: indications (late pregnancy, cannot encircle) confirmed in the AHA-ABLS cpr.heart.org page (Class 1, C-EO); ARC-LAY indications (obviously or known to be pregnant, too large to wrap your arms around). Wheelchair, corrected in round 3 (ARC-LAY re-read 2026-11-17): give abdominal thrusts as usual, kneeling behind the wheelchair and removing armrests if needed; chest thrusts only if abdominal thrusts are difficult. s7 and s8 now say this. Hand position (arms around chest, fist on the middle of the breastbone between the nipples, firm backward thrusts) from MedlinePlus, read 2026-11-17. This detail comes from outside the AHA/Red Cross tier, because neither publishes a hand position on a page that could be opened. See open questions.
  - 709 persons cohort — confirmed 2026-11-17 on the cpr.heart.org AHA-ABLS page, FBAO supporting text section 10.1. Checked twice.
  - Self-rescue: own abdominal thrusts, or press the abdomen against a firm object such as the back of a chair; not anything with a sharp edge or corner — ARC-LAY, re-read 2026-11-17 (replaces "edge of a table").
- Notes: Edition 2025. Self-treatment (own fist or chair back) from AHA-ABLS supporting text. Unresponsive: CPR starting with compressions, remove visible object only, no blind sweeps (Class 3: Harm). Infant branch ends with "Use the Choking, infant protocol".

## choking-infant
- Sources read: AHA-PBLS 2025 FBAO (recommendations and supporting text); AHA-HL 2025; ARC-LAY infant choking.
- Numbers checked:
  - 5 back blows alternating with 5 chest thrusts — AHA-PBLS (Class 1). Checked twice.
  - Heel of one hand for chest thrusts — AHA-PBLS supporting text and AHA-HL. Checked twice.
  - About 1.5 inches deep — chest thrust depth — ARC-LAY infant choking. Read once (AHA gives no thrust depth).
  - Under about 1 year — AHA-PBLS. Checked twice.
- Review fixes: back-blow step now says to stop as soon as the object comes out (ARC-LAY "continue until the infant can cough or cry or becomes unresponsive"); help wording made exact.
- Notes: No abdominal thrusts in infants (AHA-PBLS, AHA-HL). Positioning (face down on forearm braced on thigh, head lower than chest, jaw supported; face up for thrusts) from ARC-LAY. See open questions for the two-finger vs heel-of-hand difference.

## recovery-position
- Sources read: ARC-DB Positioning (last full review 2024, page modified 2025-11-25); ARC-DB Spinal injury (ILCOR 2025); AHA-FA 2024 section 5.1; ARC-LAY unresponsive and breathing person.
- Numbers checked: none (no numeric values in this protocol).
- Notes: Recovery position for decreased responsiveness of nontraumatic cause with normal breathing (AHA-FA Class 2a). Continuous monitoring (good practice). Reposition supine if the position prevents assessment. Suspected neck, back, hip or pelvis injury: leave in position found unless unsafe (AHA-FA Class 1 for responsive persons; ARC-LAY: unless moving for safety, CPR or bleeding). Lay responders should not apply collars, manipulate the neck or strap the head (ARC-DB). Face-down or flexed casualties rolled supine to reassess: ILCOR 2026 CoSTR FA 7040 good-practice statement (Table 22, preprint May 2026, https://ilcor.org/uploads/MERGED-2026_ILCOR-CoSTR_May-2026-for-Preprint.pdf) and ARC-DB Positioning; conflict with ARC-LAY spine advice recorded in open questions. Technique (one arm up under head, other hand to face, knees bent, left side preferred) from ARC-LAY instructor description; left side in late pregnancy from ARC-DB evidence summary.

## anaphylaxis
- Sources read: AHA-FA 2024 section 6.5; ARC-DB Anaphylaxis (page modified 2025-11-21); ARC-LAY allergic reaction/anaphylaxis.
- Numbers checked:
  - 5 to 10 minutes — second dose timing — ARC-LAY; AHA-FA (EMS more than 5 to 10 min). Checked twice.
  - 7% to 18% need more than one dose — AHA-FA 6.5 supportive text item 4, re-read 2026-11-17. Used in the protocol. ARC-DB gives 18 to 35 percent (5 to 15 minutes); recorded in open questions, not used.
  - 90 degrees, hold 3 seconds after click, massage 10 seconds — auto-injector technique — ARC-LAY. Read once (lay page); detail tells users to follow the device's printed instructions if different.
  - Over about 20 minutes delay linked to more deaths — ARC-DB background (Kemp 2008). Not in the visible AHA-FA text (second pass "not found" there).
  - Timer 300 seconds — earliest second dose, from the 5-minute lower bound.
- Review fixes: s6 now tells the user to wait for the 5-minute timer before answering; help wording in s9 made exact; ARC-DB source label corrected to "last full review 2020; intranasal review 2025".
- Notes: Dose amounts are not stated; the user gives the device as prescribed (rule 5). Intranasal epinephrine (neffy) mentioned per ARC-DB/ARC-LAY. Positioning: sit comfortably, lie down if signs of shock (ARC-LAY).

## chest-wound
- Sources read: AHA-FA 2024 section 9.2 (recommendations and supporting text); ARC-DB Open chest wounds (last full review 2023); TCCC 2026 Respiration/Breathing 5a-5b.
- Numbers checked: none numeric in the protocol.
- Notes: Open chest wound is a medical emergency requiring EMS activation (AHA-FA Class 1). Leave open, clean nonocclusive dry dressing, or vented chest seal (AHA-FA Class 2a). Monitor and loosen or remove dressing if breathing worsens (AHA-FA Class 1). Small stab/handgun wounds often do not impair breathing (AHA-FA supporting text). TCCC chest-seal wording confirmed in the TCCC 2026 guidelines text (allogy book, opened 2026-11-16, Tactical Field Care 5b): "All open and/or sucking chest wounds should be treated by immediately applying a vented chest seal... If a vented chest seal is not available, use a non-vented chest seal... treat by burping or removing the dressing or by needle decompression." ARC-DB source year corrected to 2023 (last full review). TCCC tension pneumothorax signs (progressive respiratory distress, rapid breathing, shock) used for the "breathing worse" check. Needle decompression: "see Advanced procedures". See open questions for vented vs unvented and open-wound guidance.

## shock
- Sources read: AHA-FA 2024 section 5.2; ARC-DB Position for shock (ILCOR 2015, update 2024); ARC-LAY Shock; TCCC 2026 (hemorrhagic shock check, hypothermia prevention).
- Numbers checked:
  - Raise feet about 6 to 12 inches (about 30 to 45 degrees), only with no trauma — AHA-FA (Class 2b); ARC-DB; ARC-LAY "about 6 to 12 inches". Checked twice.
  - Leg-raise benefit transient, under 7 minutes — ARC-DB evidence summary. Written as "only a few minutes". Read once.
- Notes: Supine if alert (AHA-FA 2a); recovery position if at risk of airway obstruction or cannot be continuously watched (AHA-FA 2a). Nothing by mouth, keep temperature normal, reassure (ARC-LAY). Leg raising was once routine; current guidance makes it optional and only without injury.

ARC-DB (firstaidguide.wpenginepowered.com) refused the fetch (robots), so nothing from ARC-DB is used in this batch.

Not used, could not be opened: WMS Clinical Practice Guideline on Care of Burns in the Wilderness, 2025 (Bitter et al., Wilderness Environ Med 2025;36(4):549-558, PMID 40534479) — PubMed returned an empty page and SAGE refused; any newer WMS friction-blister guideline (SAGE refused); StatPearls Rule of Nines (NBK513287, reCAPTCHA).

## Burn-area tool tables (BURN_TABLES in src/emergency-tools.js)
- Adult table in the app: head 9, left arm 9, right arm 9, chest 9, abdomen 9, upper back 9, lower back 9, left leg 18, right leg 18, groin 1 (total 100).
  - SP-BURN: head 9%, each arm 9%, anterior chest and abdomen 18%, posterior chest and back 18%, each leg 18%, perineum 1%. ACI-BURN: head 9, each arm 9, front torso 18, back torso 18, each leg 18, perineum 1.
  - Result: MATCHES both. The sources give the front and back of the trunk as 18% each; the app's split into chest 9 + abdomen 9 and upper back 9 + lower back 9 is the conventional halving and sums to the same. No change needed.
- Child table in the app: head 18, each arm 9, front of body 18, back of body 18, each leg 14 (total 100).
  - ACI-BURN, chart labelled "For children less than 9 years": head 18, each arm 9, front torso 18, back torso 18, each leg 14. Per-year adjustment: for every year of life, take 1% from the head and add 0.5% to each leg. Result: MATCHES ACI exactly.
  - SP-BURN: "For children, the head is 18%, and the legs are 13.5% each" (no arm or trunk values given). Differs from the app by 0.5% per leg; ACI (which gives the complete table) is used.
  - Age: the app's child numbers are the proportions at birth to about 1 year. ACI says to adjust by 1% head and 0.5% per leg per year of age, and labels the chart for children under 9. At age 5, for example, ACI gives head 13 and each leg 16.5. The app does not adjust by age; the burns protocol `notes` (shown above the figure) now tell the user this. Suggested code change for the controller, optional: either label the child mode "baby (under 1)" or add an age adjustment; no table numbers need correcting.
- Palm method: ACI-BURN and SP-BURN: the patient's whole hand (palm and fingers) is about 1%; SP-BURN: palm without fingers about 0.5%.
- Depths counted: SP-BURN: only partial-thickness and full-thickness burns are counted; ACI-BURN: ignore simple erythema (epidermal burns). Both used in `notes`.
- Checked twice (second fetch of each page on 2026-11-17 quoted the same figures).

## march
- Sources read: TCCC-ASM (2019); TCCC-CLS (2020); TCCC 2026 TFC 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 14, 17; AHA-FA 9.4, 9.5.
- Numbers checked:
  - 10 minutes — cannot follow simple instructions after head injury = possible moderate/severe TBI — TCCC 2026 TFC 8b. Checked twice.
  - 5 hours — surgery within 5 hours improves TBI outcome — TCCC 2026 TFC 8b. Checked twice.
  - 2 to 3 inches (in Severe bleeding, referred to) — TCCC TFC 3a. Checked twice.
- Notes: Order M-A-R-C-H and the lay actions at each letter from TCCC-ASM (blood sweep; head tilt-chin lift or jaw thrust; seated or recovery position; check shock and bleeding; keep warm and dry; head injury signs). The ASM sheet puts head injury as a separate step 5; the CLS notes and the task brief put it under H, as here. TCCC 2026 TFC 4c/4d: conscious casualty any position, unconscious recovery position. Shock signs: TCCC TFC 6b (altered mental status without brain injury, weak or absent radial pulse) and TCCC-ASM (fast breathing, losing focus, cool clammy skin, pale or grey). Hypothermia: TCCC TFC 7a-d. CPR: TCCC TFC 17 vs AHA, open question. Each letter refers by exact title to Severe bleeding, Recovery position, CPR adult/child/infant, Open chest wound, Shock, Head injury and concussion, Broken bone (fracture), Burns, Wound cleaning and care. No blind finger sweeps (TCCC-CLS).

## head-injury
- Sources read: AHA-FA 9.5; CDC-TBI; ARC-LAY Concussion; ARC-LAY Head Neck and Spinal Injury; TCCC 2026 TFC 8.
- Numbers checked:
  - 10 minutes, 5 hours — TCCC TFC 8b. Checked twice.
  - "within a couple of weeks" — most people feel better — CDC-TBI. Checked twice.
- Notes: Remove from activity, no return until evaluated by a health care professional (AHA 9.5, Class 1 B-NR). Activate EMS for loss of consciousness, worsening headache, vomiting, altered mental status, seizures, visual changes, scalp swelling or deformity (AHA 9.5, Class 1 C-EO). Adult and child danger signs from CDC-TBI (re-read word for word on the second pass). Bruising around eyes or behind ears, blood or fluid from nose or ears: ARC-LAY Head Neck and Spinal. No painkillers for the headache: ARC-LAY (both pages). Sleep: ARC-LAY Concussion FAQ, open question. The sources give no interval for repeat checks; the protocol says so rather than inventing one.

## spine-injury
- Sources read: AHA-FA 9.4; ARC-LAY Head Neck and Spinal Injury; WMS-SPINE (abstract only); MLP Spinal injury; TCCC 2026 TFC 4h.
- Numbers checked: none numeric.
- Notes: Change in guidance recorded as asked: AHA-FA 2024 9.4 — stay as still as possible unless safety warrants movement, activate EMS (Class 1, C-EO); routine rigid cervical collars and long spine boards by first aid providers are not recommended (Class 3: Harm, C-LD); routine spinal immobilization not recommended for penetrating trauma (Class 3: Harm, C-LD). WMS-SPINE abstract: goal-oriented care, no rigid collars or backboards for suspected spine injuries. TCCC TFC 4h: no cervical stabilization for penetrating trauma only. Moving and rolling: AHA (safety), ARC-LAY (CPR, bleeding), MLP (vomiting or choking on blood, two or more people, roll as a unit); see open questions. Helmet on and child in car seat unless CPR: ARC-LAY. The WMS field spine-clearance criteria could not be read and are not included.

## fracture
- Sources read: AHA-FA 9.7; ARC-LAY Fractures; ARC-LAY Muscle Bone and Joint Injury; MLP Broken bone; MLP How to make a splint; TCCC 2026 TFC 16; TCCC-CLS; WMS-WOUND (open fractures).
- Numbers checked:
  - 20 minutes on, 20 minutes off — cold pack — ARC-LAY Fractures, Muscle Bone and Joint. Checked three times. The 10 on, 10 off fallback is from ARC-LAY Wounds, General Care: Bruise (re-read 2026-11-17).
  - About 2 seconds — capillary refill — MLP Broken bone. Checked twice.
- Notes: Blue, purple or pale limb → activate EMS immediately (AHA 9.7, Class 1 C-EO; checked twice). Splinting useful (2a), position found unless straightening needed for transport (2b), cover open wounds with clean dressing (2b). Joints above and below: TCCC-CLS and MLP splint page. Check circulation before and after: TCCC-CLS ("check pulses before and after splinting"); TCCC 2026 TFC 16 says only "re-check pulses". Pelvis is life-threatening; rib care with a pillow: ARC-LAY. Hip/pelvis/thigh move by clothes only if necessary: MLP Broken bone. Open fractures need antibiotics (WMS-WOUND 1A) and are an evacuation criterion (WMS 1C); antibiotics themselves are not given (content rule 5). Realignment: open question.

## sprain-dislocation
- Sources read: AHA-FA 9.6, 9.7; ARC-LAY Muscle Bone and Joint Injury, Fractures, Wounds; MLP Broken bone (sling).
- Numbers checked:
  - 20 to 30 minutes per application — AHA 9.6 (Class 2a, C-LD). Checked twice.
  - 3 to 4 times a day — AHA 9.6 supportive text (expert suggestion). Checked twice.
  - 20 minutes on, 20 off — ARC-LAY Muscle Bone and Joint, Fractures; 10/10 if not tolerated — ARC-LAY Wounds, Bruise section. Checked three times.
  - About half the width overlap — compression wrap — ARC-LAY Muscle Bone and Joint. Read once.
- Notes: Current wording used as asked: AHA/Red Cross 2024 do not use RICE; they recommend avoiding activity that causes pain and seeking medical attention (Class 1), cold for pain and swelling (2a), compression wrap for an acute ankle sprain for comfort (2b, no evidence it reduces swelling or speeds recovery), and say nothing about elevation. Ice-and-water bag in a damp cloth cools best (AHA supportive text). No heat on a fresh injury: ARC-LAY. Reduction: "see Advanced procedures".

## burns
- Sources read: AHA-FA 9.8, 8.2; ARC-LAY Burns; TCCC 2026 TFC 7, 15; WMS-WOUND (burn section, evacuation criteria); SP-BURN; ACI-BURN.
- Numbers checked:
  - 5 to 20 minutes — cooling time — AHA 9.8 (Class 2b, B-NR); ARC-LAY. Checked twice in each.
  - Cool or cold clean running water; lukewarm only if cool not available — water temperature — AHA 9.8 supportive text; ARC-LAY. Checked twice. Neither gives degrees.
  - At least 15 minutes — chemical skin flushing — AHA 8.2 (Class 1, B-NR); ARC-LAY. Checked twice.
  - At least 15 minutes — electrical burn cooling — ARC-LAY. Checked twice.
  - 10 minutes maximum — wrapped ice, superficial burns with intact skin only — AHA 9.8 (2b, C-LD) and supportive text. Checked twice.
  - Preadolescent children — hypothermia monitoring during cooling — AHA 9.8 (Class 1, C-LD). Checked twice.
  - More than 10% adults, 5% children — usually burn centre care — AHA 9.8 supportive text. Checked twice.
  - Larger than the person's palm — partial-thickness burn needing evaluation — AHA 9.8 (Class 1, B-NR). Checked twice.
  - 20% — TCCC threshold for heat-reflective shell and IV fluids — TCCC TFC 15d/e. Checked twice.
  - Nearest 10% — TCCC TBSA estimate — TCCC TFC 15c. Checked twice.
  - 1% whole hand, 0.5% palm only — SP-BURN, ACI-BURN. Checked twice.
- Notes: `tool: "burn"` on s6 (size estimate). `evacuate` holds the AHA criteria (full-thickness; partial-thickness larger than palm or on face, hands, feet, genitals; smoke inhalation signs; burn centre thresholds), ARC-LAY 911 and hospital lists (joints, circumferential, electrical, chemical, radiation, explosion, very young or old, other conditions, any burn on a child), WMS-WOUND (infected burns, uncontrolled pain) and TCCC (over 20%). `notes` hold which depths count, the hand rule, the child-figure age note and TCCC rounding. What not to put on a burn: ARC-LAY (butter, mayonnaise, petroleum jelly, adhesive bandages, ice). Jewellery removal: AHA (Class 1, C-EO) and ARC-LAY. Do not clean a severe burn: ARC-LAY. Dry chemicals brushed off first (AHA 2a); hydrofluoric acid and phenol need other agents (AHA supportive). Electrical: power off, cool at least 15 minutes, evaluate even small burns, be ready for CPR and AED (ARC-LAY). Over-the-counter pain medicine reasonable (AHA 9.8, 2a); no dose given, "as the package directs". WMS burn guideline 2025 not read (see above).

## eye-injury
- Sources read: AHA-FA 8.3, 9.10; ARC-LAY Eye Injury; MLP Eye emergencies; TCCC 2026 TFC 9; TCCC-CLS.
- Numbers checked:
  - 15 minutes — chemical eye irrigation with copious tap water — AHA 8.3 (Class 1, C-LD); MLP "at least 15 minutes". Checked twice.
- Notes: Irrigate immediately (AHA Class 1); saline, Ringer's or eyewash if immediately available (2a); avoid contaminating other eye and others (Class 1). Head tilted so injured eye is lower: ARC-LAY, MLP. Penetrating or high-velocity injury, irregular pupil, bleeding, vision loss → immediate care (AHA 9.10 Class 1). Do not rub (Class 1); tape a hard shield or paper cup (Class 1, C-EO). TCCC: rigid shield, not a pressure patch; rapid visual acuity check. Low-energy particles: tears or rinse (2a). Contact lens with foreign-body sensation: remove, stop, seek care (Class 1). Blow or cut: cool compress, no pressure on the eyeball (MLP). Eyelid cuts may reach the eyeball (MLP).

## amputation
- Sources read: MLP Amputation - traumatic; TCCC 2026 TFC 3a; AHA-FA 9.1.
- Numbers checked:
  - About 4 to 6 hours — reattachment window without cooling — MLP. Checked twice.
  - 2 to 3 inches above the wound — TCCC TFC 3a. Checked twice.
- Notes: Part care exactly per MLP: remove visible debris, rinse gently, wrap in clean damp cloth, seal in plastic bag, place bag in ice water bath; not directly on ice, not in water without a bag, no dry ice; if no cold water keep away from heat. No AHA or Red Cross page that could be opened covers the amputated part, so MLP (outside the tier) is the only source for it. Elevation (MLP) left out: AHA/ARC say elevation does not help control bleeding. Tourniquet: open question.

## impaled-object
- Sources read: ARC-LAY Bleeding - Life-Threatening External (embedded or impaled objects); MLP Cuts and puncture wounds; ARC-LAY Abdominal Injury.
- Numbers checked: none numeric.
- Notes: Do not remove (removal can make bleeding much worse); pressure around the object; several dressings around it, bulky dressings or roller bandages, bandaged in place; tourniquet above the object if needed (ARC-LAY). MLP: do not remove a long or deeply stuck object. No source opened gives exceptions (open question).

## abdominal-wound
- Sources read: ARC-LAY Abdominal Injury; ARC-LAY Bleeding - Life-Threatening External; ARC-LAY Shock (nothing by mouth, via the Shock protocol); AHA-FA 9.1.
- Numbers checked: none numeric.
- Notes: Consistent with bleeding s17: no pushing organs back, no direct pressure on them, moist dressings with clean warm tap water or saline laid loosely, plastic wrap or foil loosely over them, back with knees bent unless painful, leave as found with possible spine injury, shock care, stay with them. Packing is not instructed (no lay-level source supports packing a belly wound; ARC lists packing "if trained" for the trunk — existing open question). Signs list from ARC-LAY Abdominal Injury.

## wound-care
- Sources read: AHA-FA 9.3; WMS-WOUND; MLP Cuts and puncture wounds; CDC-TET.
- Numbers checked:
  - At least 1 litre — irrigation volume, for wound irrigation in general (not only dirty wounds) — WMS-WOUND (1C). Checked twice (both WMS copies).
  - About 8 psi from a 35 mL syringe with a 19-gauge needle — WMS-WOUND. Checked twice.
  - 5 minutes — rinse a minor puncture under running water — MLP. Checked twice.
  - A quarter inch (0.64 cm) deep — may need stitches — MLP. Checked twice.
  - 1 to 12% — infection rate with proper care — WMS-WOUND. Checked twice.
  - 24 to 48 hours — start rabies prophylaxis — WMS-WOUND. Checked twice.
  - 10 years / 5 years — tetanus booster for clean / other wounds after 3 or more doses — WMS-WOUND table; CDC-TET 10-year boosters. Checked twice.
  - 3 to 21 days — tetanus symptom onset — CDC-TET. Checked twice.
- Notes: Irrigate until no visible debris (AHA Class 1); tap water or saline (2a); occlusive dressing (2a); signs to remove dressing and seek care (2a); bites to a medical facility (Class 1). WMS: potable water, high pressure, no additives except povidone-iodine for rabies-prone wounds, clip not shave, remove easy organic foreign bodies, leave contaminated wounds open, moist dressing, evacuation list. Wound closure (WMS time limits 6 h, 10 h face and scalp) is mentioned only as "see Advanced procedures"; the time limits are not in the protocol. Antibiotics are not given (rule 5).

## blisters
- Sources read: WMS-WOUND (friction blisters); NHS-BLISTER.
- Numbers checked:
  - 5 mm — threshold for draining — WMS-WOUND (1C). Checked twice.
- Notes: Prevention (footwear, dry feet, wicking socks, 1C; antiperspirant one study; weak evidence for moleskin, tape, benzoin, lubricants, powders), hot spots and small blisters padded or covered with hydrocolloid or hydrogel, large blisters drained not unroofed and covered, no tissue glue — all WMS-WOUND. Infection signs and hygiene: NHS. A newer WMS blister guideline could not be opened. Red Cross lay pages have no blister topic.

## Task 11 path check
Every path through every protocol was listed with a script (34 paths in march, 9 in fracture, 8 in abdominal-wound, 5 in burns, 4 in eye-injury, 3 in head-injury, 2 each in spine-injury, sprain-dislocation, wound-care, blisters, 1 each in amputation and impaled-object) and each instruction was checked against the casualties who can reach it. Changes made from that check: march s5 warn (jaw thrust with possible neck injury); march s12 (sit up if needed to breathe); march s13 detail (roll as a unit to insulate a possible spine injury); fracture s8/s11 (no "position found" contradiction after the poor-circulation step; ribs and pelvis excluded from splinting in the action); impaled-object s2 (object in the eye goes to the Eye injury protocol before any pressure); blisters s4 (ointment under gauze only for drained blisters). Belly-organ warnings are on every pressure step reachable with an evisceration (march s3, impaled-object s3, abdominal-wound s9).

- Numbers re-checked: ACI, for every year of life take 1% from the head and add 0.5% to each leg, chart covers 0 to 8 years (burns notes). AAD has no numbers. TCCC 7d: heating blanket on the front of the torso and in the armpits, never directly on the skin, not wrapped around the torso (march s13, now attributed; the reviewer's "could not verify" item is resolved). MedlinePlus Fishhook removal: no numbers.
- New sources added to files: recovery-position (MedlinePlus Spinal injury, Mayo Clinic), spine-injury, march and head-injury (Mayo and/or MedlinePlus Spinal injury), impaled-object (MedlinePlus Fishhook removal, Oregon EMS manual), blisters (AAD).
- Path check redone on every touched protocol (march, head-injury, spine-injury, fracture, burns, amputation, impaled-object, abdominal-wound, wound-care, blisters, recovery-position); see the task report for the result.

## Task 11 review fixes, round 2 (2026-11-17)
Re-review: task-11-content-rereview.md (N1 Important, N2 to N5 Minor). Pages re-opened: MedlinePlus Cuts and puncture wounds (major wounds), Fishhook removal, How to make a splint, Broken bone; Red Cross Abdominal Injury.
- N1/N2 wound-care: s10 ask now reads "...to stop heavy bleeding". Its No goes to s12 "Are organs showing through the wound?" (yes: s14, do not rinse, Belly (abdominal) injury protocol, evacuate) and then s13 "Is the wound on the chest or back, over the ribs?" (yes: s15, do not rinse, Open chest wound protocol, evacuate). Red Cross re-read: organs get moist dressings only, with no rinsing; MedlinePlus: do not clean a major wound.
- N3/N4 fracture s8: action "Do not try to reposition a hip, pelvis or thigh injury"; warn includes the thigh; detail credits both MedlinePlus pages with each page's own wording of the exception.
- N5 impaled-object: s8 wire-cutting only when the tip lies near the surface of the skin (MedlinePlus), otherwise the fishing-line method; evacuate list now excepts a superficial fishhook removed as in the fishhook step.
- Path check redone on wound-care (5 paths), fracture (9) and impaled-object (3): irrigation is now reachable only by wounds that needed no haemorrhage control, have no organs showing, and are not over the ribs; repositioning is excluded on the hip, pelvis and thigh path; wire-cutting is reachable only for a superficial hook with its tip near the surface.

## Task 11 review fixes, round 3 (2026-11-17)
- M1 wound-care s13: the question is now "Could the wound go through the chest wall?" The cues are in the detail: stab, gunshot, something stuck in or through it, deep, bubbling or sucking. A graze or shallow cut on the trunk goes on to irrigation (AHA-FA 9.3: irrigate superficial wounds and abrasions thoroughly). s15 is unchanged.
- M2 impaled-object s8: the action now ends "Otherwise: fishing-line method (see detail)." (187 characters).

## Task 12 source keys
Sources first read 2026-11-17 and re-opened for the second pass the same day (each number below was confirmed on the second opening unless marked).
- AHA-FA 7.7 to 7.10: AHA and American Red Cross, 2024 Guidelines for First Aid, sections 7.7 Hypothermia, 7.8 Frostbite, 7.9 Heat stroke and exertional hyperthermia, 7.10 Oral rehydration for exertional dehydration. https://cpr.heart.org/en/resuscitation-science/2024-first-aid-guidelines (page read at offsets 90,000 to 190,000).
- AHA-SPEC 11, 12, 16, 17: AHA 2025 Part 10 Special Circumstances, sections 11 Drowning, 12 Electrocution, 16 Hyperthermia, 17 Hypothermia. https://cpr.heart.org/en/resuscitation-science/cpr-and-ecc-guidelines/adult-and-pediatric-special-circumstances-of-resuscitation. No lightning or standalone carbon monoxide section exists in Part 10 (checked all three page segments).
- WMS-HYPO: WMS Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Out-of-Hospital Evaluation and Treatment of Accidental Hypothermia: 2019 Update (Dow et al., WEM 2019;30(4S):S47-S69), full text via https://www.femecv.com/sites/default/files/WMS%20hipotermia_2019.pdf. A WMS listing (wmsdev.imiscloud.com, 2023) shows 2019 as current; no later edition found by search.
- WMS-HEAT: WMS magazine, "Heat Illness CPG", summary of the WMS heat illness guideline 2024 update. https://wms.imiscloud.com/magazine/1540/Heat-Illness-CPG/default.aspx. The full 2024 text was not opened.
- WMS-DROWN: WMS magazine, "2024 Drowning Summary", summary of the WMS drowning guideline 2024 update. https://wms.org/magazine/1503/2024-Drowning-Summary/default.aspx (also at wms.imiscloud.com). PubMed 38379489 failed to load; full text not opened.
- WMS-LT14: WMS, "A Summary: WMS Practice Guidelines for the Prevention and Treatment of Lightning Injuries" (Engeln), summarising the 2014 update (Davis et al., WEM 2014;25:S86-S95). https://wms.org/common/Uploaded%20files/Magazine/Lightning%20summary%20post%20edit.pdf
- WMS-LT12: WMS lightning guideline 2012 (Davis et al., WEM 2012;23:260-269), superseded; full text via https://clinicalmonster.com/wp-content/uploads/2013/04/lightning.pdf; plus the 2012 WMS news release (EurekAlert, https://www.eurekalert.org/news-releases/917277) for the reverse-triage rationale.
- Not opened (recorded so no one cites them): WMS frostbite 2024 update (PubMed 38577729 abstract only; sagepub full text and PDF client error); WMS acute altitude illness 2024 update (PMC11855094 review blocked by a captcha; Europe PMC client error); WMS lightning 2014 full text (wemjournal client error); Red Cross lay Drowning and Poisoning pages (client error); CDC extreme-heat warning-signs page (client error).
- ARC-LAY (read 2026-11-17): redcross.org learn-first-aid pages Hypothermia, Frostbite, Heat Stroke, Heat Exhaustion (with heat cramps), Dehydration.
- CDC-HYPO: CDC Preventing Hypothermia, https://www.cdc.gov/winter-weather/prevention/index.html (last reviewed 25 Aug 2026).
- MLP: MedlinePlus Frostbite (000057, reviewed 2025-10-14); How to prevent frostbite and hypothermia (000866, reviewed 2026-07-15); Dehydration (000982, reviewed 2025-07-01); Non-fatal drowning (000046, reviewed 2025-10-14); Carbon monoxide poisoning (002804, reviewed 2025-01-08). All outside the AHA/Red Cross tier.
- NIOSH: CDC/NIOSH Heat-related Illnesses, https://www.cdc.gov/niosh/heat-stress/about/illnesses.html (last reviewed 3 Mar 2026).
- CDC-TD: CDC Yellow Book 2026, Travelers' Diarrhea (dated 24 Mar 2026). WHO-ORS: WHO EMRO, Information for the general public on oral rehydration solution, WHO-EM/CSR/594/E (undated), https://applications.emro.who.int/docs/WHOEMCSR594E-eng.pdf
- CDC-ALT: CDC Yellow Book 2026, High-Altitude Travel and Altitude Illness (Hackett and Shlim, dated 23 Apr 2025). JTS-ALT: Joint Trauma System CPG ID 95, Altitude Emergencies in the Prehospital Environment, 05 Mar 2024 (written for military medical providers).
- CDC-LT: CDC Safety Guidelines: Lightning (with first aid), https://www.cdc.gov/lightning/safety/ (last reviewed 16 Jun 2026).
- CDC-CO: CDC Carbon Monoxide Poisoning Basics (last reviewed 26 Oct 2026). CPSC-CO: CPSC Portable Heaters and Camping Equipment (undated). CDC-WS: CDC Safety Guidelines: During and After a Winter Storm (last reviewed 25 Aug 2026).

## hypothermia
- Sources read: AHA-FA 7.7; AHA-SPEC 17; WMS-HYPO; ARC-LAY Hypothermia; CDC-HYPO.
- Numbers checked: 1 minute — carotid pulse check before CPR in hypothermia — WMS-HYPO resuscitation (used for the breathing check, s1/s15; see open question).
- Numbers checked: about 30 minutes — insulated, warm rest before a shivering patient stands or walks — WMS-HYPO ("should last 30 min but will depend upon the situation").
- Numbers checked: 20 to 30 minutes — interval for checking heated skin for burns — WMS-HYPO.
- Numbers checked: no more than 10 minutes first delay; at least 5 minutes of CPR between pauses of no more than 5 minutes — delayed/intermittent CPR — WMS-HYPO.
- Numbers checked: 6 hours 30 minutes — longest CPR with good recovery reported — WMS-HYPO.
- Numbers checked: about 86 F (30 C) — shivering stops at and below — WMS-HYPO ("approximately 30C"); 30 C = 86.0 F.
- Numbers checked: 6 hours — transport to an ECLS centre may be reasonable for hypothermic cardiac arrest (2b) — AHA-SPEC 17.
- Numbers checked: below 95 F (35 C) — definition of hypothermia — ARC-LAY Hypothermia, CDC-HYPO (not used in steps; context only).
- Notes: heat-pack order armpits, chest, back (WMS 1B); limbs excluded (AHA 3: Harm "heat sources, rubbing, and massage should not be applied to the extremities"). Warm shower or immersion potentially harmful with decreased responsiveness (AHA 3: Harm). Drinks: AHA "alert and can safely consume oral food or fluids", high-calorie; WMS warm high-carbohydrate, not hot; Red Cross warm non-caffeinated, never alcohol. Mild uninjured alert shivering patient may be treated without transport (WMS 1B). Gentle handling, horizontal, limit exertion (WMS 1B). Two vapour barriers (WMS 1C). Charcoal Heat Pac outdoors or monitored ventilation (WMS 1B). Red Cross asterisk sign "loss of ability to shiver as condition worsens" supports s3b. Consistent with march s13 (TCCC chest and armpits). AHA-SPEC defibrillation (once, then defer until 30 C (86 F)) is not given to lay users beyond "follow the AED prompts".

## frostbite
- Sources read: AHA-FA 7.8 (and 7.7 for core rewarming first); ARC-LAY Frostbite; CDC-HYPO (frostbite part); MLP Frostbite; MLP How to prevent frostbite and hypothermia.
- Numbers checked: 99 to 104 F (37 to 40 C) — lukewarm rewarming water — AHA-FA 7.8 recommendation 1 (Class 1); ARC-LAY steps give the same.
- Numbers checked (review round 1): 98.6 to 102.2 F (37 to 39 C) — WMS 2019 field rewarming water, with a thermometer — AAFP 2020 summary (https://www.aafp.org/pubs/afp/issues/2020/0401/p440.html) and Medical Dialogues 2019 summary (https://speciality.medicaldialogues.in/frostbite-treatment-and-prevention-new-practice-guidelines). App uses the overlap 99 to 102 F (37 to 39 C); 39 C = 102.2 F, shown as 102 F.
- Numbers checked (review round 1): 30 seconds — uninjured extremity in the water to test it without a thermometer — AAFP 2020 (does not say hand; the app says hand or foot). Typically within 30 minutes — rewarming complete — AAFP and Medical Dialogues. More than 2 hours from definitive care — field rapid rewarming — AAFP and Medical Dialogues.
- Numbers checked: 113 F (45 C) — water harmful in animal study — AHA-FA 7.8 supportive text.
- Numbers checked: 20 to 30 minutes — immersion time in human case series — AHA-FA 7.8 (timer 1800 s).
- Numbers checked: slightly warmer than body temperature on the wrist — no-thermometer test — AHA-FA 7.8.
- Numbers checked (open question only): 100 to 105 F (38 to 40.5 C) — ARC-LAY FAQ; 104 to 108 F (40 to 42.2 C) for 15 to 30 minutes — MLP Frostbite.
- Notes: ibuprofen AHA 2b, no dose. No debridement of blisters (AHA 3: No Benefit); do not break (Red Cross); do not disturb (MLP). Avoid walking on frozen feet when avoidable (AHA); unless necessary (CDC). Rewarm only if no risk of refreezing (AHA, Red Cross, MLP). No rubbing (Red Cross, CDC, MLP). No dry heat (CDC, MLP). Frostnip from MLP only. The wound-care tetanus pointer cites the Wound cleaning and care protocol, not a source.

## heat-illness
- Sources read: AHA-FA 7.9 (and 7.10 for drinks); AHA-SPEC 16; WMS-HEAT; ARC-LAY Heat Stroke and Heat Exhaustion; NIOSH.
- Numbers checked: 15 minutes or until neurological symptoms resolve, whichever comes first — neck-down cold-water immersion — AHA-FA 7.9 (2a); ARC-LAY Heat Stroke "15 minutes or until they are no longer confused" (timer 900 s).
- Numbers checked: 36 to 79 F (2 to 26 C) — water temperatures with similar cooling rates — AHA-FA 7.9 supportive text (the source gives both units).
- Numbers checked: 102.2 F (39 C) — target core temperature for trained providers measuring it — AHA-FA 7.9 (2b).
- Numbers checked: 101.5 F (38.6 C) — stop active cooling (professionals) — AHA-SPEC 16 (2a).
- Numbers checked: 20 minutes — continue cooling (alternatives) or until condition improves or EMS personnel arrive — ARC-LAY Heat Stroke (timer 1200 s).
- Numbers checked: 15 minutes — no improvement in mild heat illness means emergent transport — WMS-HEAT (timer 900 s on s12).
- Numbers checked: 2 hours — exertional heat stroke back to baseline may be released — WMS-HEAT.
- Numbers checked: about half as fast — evaporative cooling versus immersion; about twice as fast — cheeks, palms, soles versus neck, armpits, groin — WMS-HEAT.
- Numbers checked: every 15 to 20 minutes — water plus carbohydrate-electrolyte drink for heat cramps; 1 hour — cramps not subsiding needs medical help — NIOSH.
- Notes: AHA-SPEC 16 "active cooling ... initiated concurrently with standard resuscitation" (Class 1) applies to life-threatening hyperthermia including arrest (s20). Drink to thirst; exercise-associated hyponatremia may present like heat exhaustion; no rehydration may delay cooling (WMS-HEAT). Salt tablets not effective (Red Cross FAQ), avoid (NIOSH). Red Cross: wait several hours after symptoms stop; stop fluids and recovery position if vomiting or responsiveness changes. NIOSH 106 F within 10 to 15 minutes not used. Heat syncope from NIOSH.

## dehydration
- Sources read: AHA-FA 7.10; ARC-LAY Dehydration; MLP Dehydration; CDC-TD; WHO-ORS.
- Numbers checked: 4 to 9% — carbohydrate-electrolyte drink preferred over water (2a) — AHA-FA 7.10.
- Numbers checked: 1 packet in the volume on the packet, generally 1 L — oral rehydration salts — CDC-TD.
- Numbers checked: 1 litre water, 6 teaspoons sugar, half a teaspoon salt — homemade ORS — WHO-ORS (1 litre is about 1.06 US quarts; written "about 1 quart").
- Numbers checked: 12 to 24 hours without vomiting — slowly restart a child's normal diet — ARC-LAY Dehydration.
- Numbers checked: more than 6 hours without urine (child); 3 or more hours without a wet diaper (infant) — see a provider — ARC-LAY Dehydration.
- Numbers checked: over 102 F — fever needing emergency help — MLP Dehydration. MedlinePlus prints 38.8 C; 102 F is 38.9 C, which the app now uses (review round 1, M10).
- Notes: AHA exclusions shock, confusion, inability to swallow (s2, s3 screens and warn). Red Cross gives small frequent amounts with vomiting and diarrhoea, so vomiting is not an exclusion here (s5 action says so). Red Cross "pale and odorless" urine. No salt tablets (MLP). Overly sweet drinks can cause osmotic diarrhoea (CDC-TD).

## altitude
- Sources read: CDC-ALT; JTS-ALT. (WMS altitude 2024 not opened, see keys.)
- Numbers checked: 2 to 12 hours — usual AMS onset — CDC-ALT; 1 to 6 hours, up to 48 hours — JTS-ALT.
- Numbers checked: 8,000 ft (2,450 m) — sleeping altitude risk threshold — CDC-ALT.
- Numbers checked: 3 days — symptoms starting later without further ascent are not AMS — CDC-ALT.
- Numbers checked: 1 to 3 days — AMS develops and resolves — CDC-ALT.
- Numbers checked: 14,000 ft (4,300 m) — HACE rare especially below; coma can follow within 24 hours — CDC-ALT.
- Numbers checked: at least 1,000 ft (300 m) — descent with rapid AMS improvement — CDC-ALT; 300 to 1,000 m (1,000 to 3,300 ft) — JTS-ALT (feet from CDC's own conversions).
- Numbers checked: 1,000 m (3,300 ft) — HAPE descent with early oxygen — JTS-ALT.
- Numbers checked: 1 to 2 days — rest at current altitude for isolated AMS — JTS-ALT.
- Numbers checked: 500 m (1,650 ft) a day — re-ascent once asymptomatic (JTS-ALT) and sleeping-altitude gain above 9,800 ft (3,000 m) (CDC-ALT main text says per night; its box says per day and 9,850 ft); extra day per 3,300 ft (1,000 m) — CDC-ALT.
- Numbers checked: 48 hours — mild exercise only at a new altitude — CDC-ALT.
- Numbers checked: 5,000 to 6,000 ft (1,500 to 1,800 m) — simulated descent in a pressure bag — CDC-ALT; 10 to 20 pumps a minute, 1 to 2 hour sessions, SpO2 above 90% — JTS-ALT.
- Numbers checked: about 50% — HAPE without AMS/HACE; often second night — JTS-ALT.
- Notes: no drug doses (acetazolamide, dexamethasone, nifedipine, OTC analgesics) included. JTS is a military medical-provider document; used for recognition, descent amounts and pressure-bag operation. Descent with least exertion and removing the load (JTS). Never ascend to sleep higher with symptoms (CDC).

## lightning
- Sources read: WMS-LT14 summary; CDC-LT; AHA-SPEC 12; WMS-LT12 (superseded, rationale only).
- Numbers checked: at least 20 feet — spacing between group members — WMS-LT14.
- Numbers checked: at least 30 minutes — wait after the storm before resuming outdoor activity — WMS-LT14 ("a minimum of 30 minutes"), CDC-LT (timer 1800 s).
- Numbers checked: more than 10% of body surface, burns to head or legs, pregnancy — high-risk indicators — WMS-LT14.
- Numbers checked: 2 days to 4 years — cataracts may develop — WMS-LT14.
- Numbers checked: within several hours — keraunoparalysis resolves; within 24 hours — Lichtenberg figures fade (first read only) — WMS-LT14.
- Numbers checked: up to 70 minutes — CPR with good neurological recovery after electrical injury — AHA-SPEC 12.
- Numbers checked: more than 10 miles — lightning can strike from a storm; at least 100 yards — distance from shore — CDC-LT.
- Notes: CDC says shelter near "lower trees" in a forest (corrected from "shorter" in the draft). Reverse triage (WMS-LT14: pulseless patients receive priority); rationale that ventilation support is needed early (WMS-LT12 Grade 1C; 2012 news release: a second arrest can follow if ventilation is not supported). Lightning position: crouch with feet touching, or sit on an insulated pad or pack with feet lifted (WMS-LT14); never lie flat (CDC). Removing metal objects from casualties: WMS-LT12.

## drowning
- Sources read: AHA-SPEC 11; WMS-DROWN summary; MLP Non-fatal drowning; WMS-HYPO (horizontal during water rescue).
- Numbers checked: 6 hours — observation / someone stays — WMS-DROWN.
- Numbers checked: more than 30 minutes submersion in water above 6 C (43 F), more than 90 minutes below 6 C (43 F), or 25 minutes of continuous CPR — cessation "might be reasonable" — WMS-DROWN (the source gives 43 F).
- Numbers checked: 2% to 12% — shockable rhythms after drowning (context, not in steps) — AHA-SPEC 11.
- Notes: AHA-SPEC 11: CPR with breaths (Class 1); trained may start with breaths first, not delaying compressions (2b); compression-only reasonable if unwilling, untrained or unable (2a adults, 2b children); CPR with breaths before AED (Class 1). Consistent with cpr-adult s5, cpr-child s5/s6 and cpr-infant s5. WMS-DROWN: reach, throw, row; compressions never in water; abdominal thrusts not recommended at any time; C-spine injury exceedingly rare; compression-only limited to bystanders untrained in rescue breathing; AEDs safe in wet environments. MLP: anyone who needed any resuscitation including rescue breathing alone goes to hospital; Heimlich only if airway blocked after repeated attempts (open question).

## carbon-monoxide
- Sources read: MLP Carbon monoxide poisoning; CDC-CO; CPSC-CO; CDC-WS; WMS-HYPO (charcoal heat packs).
- Numbers checked: more than 20 feet (CDC-CO) / at least 20 feet (CDC-WS) — generator distance from windows, doors and vents.
- Numbers checked: about 10 minutes per hour, one window slightly open — running a snowbound vehicle's engine and heater — CDC-WS.
- Numbers checked: 1 to 2 weeks — impaired mental ability can reappear after being symptom-free — MLP.
- Notes: move to fresh air immediately and seek immediate medical attention (MLP). Higher risk: infants, elderly, chronic heart disease, anaemia, breathing problems (CDC-CO); people at high altitude, smokers, fetus (MLP). Sleeping or drunk people can die before symptoms (CDC-CO). CDC-CO says do not use portable flameless chemical heaters indoors (it does not list them as a CO source; wording adjusted after the second pass). Never run fuel-burning portable heaters or lanterns while sleeping in enclosed areas, higher risk at altitude (CPSC-CO). AHA-FA 2024 and AHA-SPEC 2025 have no lay CO recommendation.

## Task 12 path check
Every path through each protocol was listed with a script: hypothermia 6 (after the fix), frostbite 7, heat-illness 9, dehydration 5, altitude 5, lightning 3, drowning 4, carbon-monoxide 2. Each instruction was read against every casualty who can reach it. Changes made by the check:
- hypothermia: added s3b "Have they stopped shivering while they are still cold?" after the alertness question, so an alert-seeming casualty whose shivering has stopped (Red Cross emergency sign) is not sent down the mild path (drinks, walking after 30 minutes).
- heat-illness s11: the warning for a casualty who vomits no longer says "treat as heat stroke" (vomiting alone is not heat stroke); it now says lie them on their side, keep cooling and treat it as getting worse, which leads to the 15-minute check and evacuation.
- altitude s5: detail now tells the user that early HAPE can look mild (much more breathless on exertion, new cough) and to treat breathlessness at rest as HAPE, since those casualties answer No at s4 and reach the rest-in-place path.
- lightning s23: the action no longer tells the user to crouch and sit at the same time; it gives crouch, or sit on insulation with feet lifted.
- dehydration s5: warning now also excludes a possible head injury or an injury that may need surgery (the screens before it only remove confusion, shock and overheating).
Every protocol screens for unresponsive and not breathing normally before its first instruction (drowning after the rescue step, carbon-monoxide after moving to fresh air, lightning after scene safety and calling for help), and frostbite also screens for reduced alertness (Hypothermia protocol first). Every by-mouth step (hypothermia s7, frostbite s11, heat-illness s8 and s11, dehydration s5) carries its exclusions in the action or warning.

## Task 12 review fixes (2026-11-17)
Independent review: task-12-content-review.md (1 Critical, 4 Important, 16 Minor). Sources re-opened for the fixes on 2026-11-17: AAFP 2020 WMS frostbite summary; Medical Dialogues 2019 WMS frostbite summary; AAFP 2020 WMS altitude summary; Red Cross Heat Stroke; WMS 2024 Drowning Summary; MedlinePlus Non-fatal drowning; AHA 2025 Part 10 (sections 11.3 and 16); CDC Yellow Book High-Altitude Travel.
- Tarp-assisted cooling confirmed: AHA 2025 Part 10 section 16, recommendation 3 (adults COR 2a, LOE B-R; children COR 2a, LOE C-EO): "tarp-assisted ice water cooling" when ice-water immersion is unavailable. The heat-illness s5 detail now gives this location.
- Drowning oxygen: AHA 2025 section 11.3 (COR 1, C-EO): trained rescuers should provide supplemental oxygen if available after cardiac arrest from drowning. WMS 2024: oxygen at the highest concentration available. Added to drowning s9 detail.
- Carbon monoxide: no oxygen advice added. MedlinePlus gives oxygen only as hospital care; no first-aid source was opened that gives it.
- Path check redone on all eight protocols: hypothermia 6 paths, frostbite 12, heat-illness 11, dehydration 5, altitude 5, lightning 3, drowning 4, carbon-monoxide 2. Changes made by this check:
  - frostbite s8c was reworded from "keep the feet frozen" to "do not rewarm the feet before the walk out". Casualties with already-thawed feet reach it through the new s4b blister question.
  - s8c detail now covers feet that have already thawed, and hands or face that can still be rewarmed.
- Path check, confirmations:
  - Immersion (heat-illness s5) is reached only by casualties with altered mental state. It is capped by the s5 ask and s5b.
  - Re-cooling (s7) is reached only by casualties who are thinking clearly.
  - Drinks (heat-illness s8, s11; hypothermia s7; frostbite s11; dehydration s5) all carry their exclusions.
  - Walking is excluded on the serious hypothermia path and for thawed feet (frostbite s12, s8c).
  - Rolling in drowning s6 now comes after the spine screen.
  - No abdominal thrusts or Choking pointer remain anywhere in drowning.

## Task 12 re-review follow-ups N1 to N4 (2026-11-17)
Re-review: task-12-content-rereview.md (approved; four minor items).
- N1 frostbite s8c: action now ends "Frostbitten hands or face: rewarm them now (see detail)." To stay under 200 characters, "Rewarm once they can be carried or reach care" moved to the detail, where it already is. Action length 166.
- N2 frostbite s12: action "Carry them if their feet were frostbitten, unless only the toe tips were affected (see detail)". The detail says the exception comes from one summary of WMS 2019 (Medical Dialogues 2019) and that the AAFP summary does not give it.
- N3 altitude s5 and the don't: the sedating-medicines sentence is out of the CDC paragraph and labelled as a group caution. CDC Yellow Book: short half-life hypnotics such as zolpidem 5 mg or zaleplon 5 mg are generally safe and effective, and respiratory depressants such as alcohol and opiates should not be used to aid sleep (re-read 2026-11-17 in round 1). The open question now ends with a question for Jordan.
- N4 heat-illness: s5 ask "After up to 15 minutes: are they thinking clearly now?"; s6 ask "After up to 20 minutes: are they thinking clearly now?" (matching the 20-minute timer).
- Paths unchanged: only wording changed. No branch targets moved.

## Task 13 source keys
Sources first read 2026-11-17 and re-opened for the second pass the same day; each number below was confirmed on the second opening unless marked.
- AHA-FA 7.1 to 7.6, 8.1 to 8.3, 9.3: AHA and American Red Cross, 2024 Guidelines for First Aid, sections 7.1 Bee and Wasp Stings, 7.2 Tick Bites, 7.3 Rash from Poison Ivy, Poison Oak, and Poison Sumac, 7.4 Snake Bite, 7.5 Jellyfish Stings, 7.6 Spider and Scorpion Envenomation, 8.1 Opioid Overdose, 8.2 Chemical Exposure to the Skin, 8.3 Chemical Exposure to the Eye, 9.3 Superficial Wounds (bites). https://cpr.heart.org/en/resuscitation-science/2024-first-aid-guidelines. AHA 2024 has no general poisoning (ingestion) section and does not cover rabies.
- ARC-LAY (read 2026-11-17, all undated): redcross.org learn-first-aid pages Venomous Snake Bites, Insect Bites and Stings, Tick Bites, Spider Bites, Scorpion Bites, Jellyfish Stings, Animal Bites, Poison Exposure.
- WMS-SNAKE: Wilderness Medical Society Practice Guidelines for the Treatment of Pitviper Envenomations in the United States and Canada (Kanaan et al., Wilderness Environ Med 2015;26:472-487), full text via https://core.ac.uk/download/323383135.pdf. Coral snakes are outside its scope. No newer WMS snakebite guideline was found.
- CDC-SNAKE: CDC/NIOSH Venomous Snakes at Work, https://www.cdc.gov/niosh/outdoor-workers/about/venomous-snakes.html (last reviewed 23 Sep 2026).
- CDC-TICK: CDC What to Do After a Tick Bite, https://www.cdc.gov/ticks/after-a-tick-bite/index.html (last reviewed 9 Jun 2026). CDC-TICKFS: CDC Tick Bite: What to Do, fact sheet CS310465-A (3 Nov 2021), https://www.cdc.gov/ticks/pdfs/FS_TickBite-508.pdf. CDC-RMSF: CDC About Rocky Mountain Spotted Fever (last reviewed 15 May 2024).
- CDC-INSECT: CDC/NIOSH Insects and Scorpions at Work (last reviewed 28 Sep 2026). CDC-SPIDER: CDC/NIOSH Venomous Spiders at Work (last reviewed 28 Sep 2026). CDC-PLANTS: CDC/NIOSH Poisonous Plants and Work (reviewed 1 Oct 2026).
- CDC-RAB: CDC About Rabies (updated 27 Jan 2026); CDC Clinical Screening for Rabies PEP (last reviewed 11 Sep 2026); CDC Rabies Post-exposure Prophylaxis Guidance (reviewed 10 Sep 2026); CDC Preventing Rabies from Bats (last reviewed 1 Jul 2025); CDC Yellow Book, Rabies (page dated 23 Apr 2025).
- MLP: MedlinePlus Insect bites and stings (000033, reviewed 2025-10-14); Animal bites - self-care (000734, reviewed 2026-04-13); Human bites - self-care (000736, reviewed 2026-04-13); Poisoning first aid (007579, reviewed 2025-01-08); Poison ivy - oak - sumac rash (000027, reviewed 2025-07-03). Outside the AHA/Red Cross tier.
- PC: National Capital Poison Center (Poison Control, poison.org): First aid: Act fast!; Ipecac: Don't use it; Activated charcoal: An effective treatment for poisonings (all undated, copyright 2012-2026).
- AAD: American Academy of Dermatology, Poison ivy, oak, and sumac: How to treat the rash (last updated 1 Jun 2026); not on the authority list, used for the emergency-room list and blister care.
- WMS-TICK: WMS magazine summary of the 2021 WMS tick-borne illness guideline (wmsdev.imiscloud.com); read, but it covers only prevention, so nothing from it is used.
- Not opened (recorded so no one cites them): ARC-DB pages (firstaidguide.wpenginepowered.com now robots-disallowed); MedlinePlus Animal bites encyclopedia article 000034 and Human bites 000035 (client error; the self-care pages 000734 and 000736 were used); MedlinePlus Poisoning 000003 (client error); CDC When to Seek Medical Care for Rabies (page returned no text); CDC Tickborne illness symptoms page (client error); AAD Poison ivy treatment A-Z page (client error); WMS 2021 tick-borne illness full text.

## snakebite
- Sources read: AHA-FA 7.4; ARC-LAY Venomous Snake Bites; CDC-SNAKE; WMS-SNAKE.
- Numbers checked: more than 95% — share of North American venomous bites from pit vipers — AHA-FA 7.4 background.
- Numbers checked: 10 minutes or more of walking — increased spread of an injected tracer — AHA-FA 7.4 supportive text.
- Numbers checked: minutes to hours — coral snake paralysis onset — AHA-FA 7.4 background.
- Numbers checked: 25% or more — dry bites among pit viper bites — WMS-SNAKE.
- Numbers checked: 8 hours — no symptoms by then may suggest a dry bite (observe at least 8 hours) — WMS-SNAKE (grade 1C).
- Numbers checked: grade 2C — limb at heart level — WMS-SNAKE; grade 1C — record bite time, mark leading edge (first reading only).
- Numbers checked: 1-800-222-1222 — Poison Help line — ARC-LAY Poison Exposure, MLP Poisoning first aid, PC.
- Notes: AHA classes: EMS for any venomous or possibly venomous bite (1, C-EO); rest and immobilize, minimize exertion if no delay (2a, C-LD); remove rings (2a, C-EO); ice (3: No Benefit); suction, electric shock, tourniquet, pressure immobilization (3: Harm). ARC-LAY: below the heart; no walking unless absolutely necessary; wash, clean dry dressing; do not look for or catch the snake; coral snake PIB by trained responders only. CDC-SNAKE: neutral position of comfort, rings and watches off before swelling, mark edge with time, no ice or water immersion, no alcohol, aspirin, ibuprofen or naproxen, do not drive yourself, photo from a safe distance, never handle even a dead snake or its head. WMS: do not kill or capture; evacuate to a facility with antivenom; Poison Control can help. Screens: unresponsive and not breathing normally (CPR) and severe allergic signs (Anaphylaxis) before any step; the allergy question names face/lips/tongue swelling, hives and throat closing, and its detail sends weak breathing without those signs (coral snake) down the snakebite path.

## tick
- Sources read: AHA-FA 7.2; CDC-TICK; CDC-TICKFS; CDC-RMSF; ARC-LAY Tick Bites.
- Numbers checked: 72 hours — consult a health care professional after removing an engorged tick in a high-Lyme area — AHA-FA 7.2 (Class 1, B-R).
- Numbers checked: fewer than 24 hours attached — very low Lyme risk — CDC-TICKFS.
- Numbers checked: 30 days — watch for symptoms — CDC-TICKFS; "within several days to weeks" — rash or fever, see a doctor — CDC-TICK; "several days" — monitor — ARC-LAY.
- Numbers checked: 2 to 4 days — RMSF rash after fever onset — CDC-RMSF.
- Notes: AHA: remove as soon as possible (1, B-NR); grasp the head close to the skin with tweezers or a commercial remover, pull upward with steady even pressure (1, C-LD); gasoline, nail polish, alcohol, petroleum jelly, hot match less effective; squeezing the abdomen linked to anaphylaxis. CDC: fine-tipped tweezers, else regular tweezers or fingers; do not twist or jerk; leave mouthparts that will not come out easily; clean with soap and water, rubbing alcohol or hand sanitizer; sealed container, tape, flush or alcohol; never crush with fingers; no petroleum jelly, heat, nail polish. ARC-LAY: bull's-eye rash marked as needing immediate emergency medical treatment; see a provider if mouthparts remain. No antibiotic doses are given.

## stings
- Sources read: AHA-FA 7.1 and 7.5; ARC-LAY Insect Bites and Stings and Jellyfish Stings; MLP Insect bites and stings; CDC-INSECT.
- Numbers checked: up to 60 seconds — honeybee stinger keeps delivering venom — AHA-FA 7.1.
- Numbers checked: at least 20 large hornet stings, or hundreds of honeybee stings — massive envenomation deaths — AHA-FA 7.1 ("can also occur ... generally from").
- Numbers checked: 10 minutes on, 10 minutes off — ice wrapped in a washcloth — MLP (timer 600 s).
- Numbers checked: within a day or two — fire ant bumps become pustules — CDC-INSECT.
- Numbers checked: at least 20 minutes or until pain relieved — hot-water soak for jellyfish and other marine stings — ARC-LAY Jellyfish Stings (timer 1200 s).
- Numbers checked: 40 C (104 F) — most commonly studied hot-water temperature — AHA-FA 7.5.
- Notes: AHA 7.1 classes: auto-injector use and assistance and EMS for anaphylaxis (1); eye stings evaluated (1); pluck or scrape ASAP (2a); OTC oral antihistamines and topical steroids for itching (2a); wash with soap and water (2a); OTC acetaminophen/NSAIDs (2b); ice or cold packs (2b). AHA 7.5: observe for systemic reaction, EMS for breathing difficulty, shock or severe pain (1); lift tentacles without manual contact or rinse with seawater (2a); non-scalding hot water or heat (2a); lidocaine if no hot water (2b); scraping caused more harm in a model; vinegar not recommended for or against. ARC-LAY Jellyfish: do not rub or apply an elastic bandage; 911 list (lethal jellyfish, unknown marine sting, marine allergy history, neck or face sting, shock or anaphylaxis); stingray/urchin/spiny fish flush, keep still, hot soak, tetanus. CDC-INSECT: rub off fire ants briskly; wipe gauze or scrape fingernail over a stinger, never squeeze or use tweezers; don't scratch. Fire ant detail is from CDC/NIOSH only (AHA and Red Cross do not mention fire ants). Antihistamine and pain medicine have no doses and carry the by-mouth exclusions.

## spider-scorpion
- Sources read: AHA-FA 7.6; ARC-LAY Spider Bites and Scorpion Bites; CDC-SPIDER; CDC-INSECT.
- Numbers checked: 48 hours — non-bark-scorpion symptoms usually subside — CDC-INSECT.
- Numbers checked: about an hour — brown recluse pain may develop after an hour, then a blood-filled blister — ARC-LAY Spider Bites; "one plus hours later" in the emergency-sign list.
- Numbers checked: six eyes instead of eight — brown recluse — CDC-SPIDER (first reading only).
- Notes: AHA 7.6: EMS for systemic symptoms such as breathing difficulty, rigidity, dizziness or confusion (1); seek care if pain extends beyond the site, is severe or uncontrolled by OTC medicine, an open wound develops, or systemic symptoms (1); OTC acetaminophen/NSAIDs, topical lidocaine on intact skin and ice for scorpion stings (2a); no studies of ice for spider bites; bark scorpion can impair breathing in children; black widow severe crampy pain, rigidity, sweating, hypertension; brown recluse ulcer progressing over days to weeks. ARC-LAY: reddish hourglass; violin mark; only certain if witnessed; few US spiders cause serious illness; wash, wrapped cold pack, keep area elevated and still for black widow or brown recluse until EMS; seek immediate care for scorpion stings; treat all scorpion stings as emergencies (FAQ); myths: tourniquet, cutting, suction, ice, electricity do not slow venom. CDC-INSECT: ice but not immersion in ice water; no sedatives; capture only if safe. CDC-SPIDER: wash, cold wet cloth or ice cloth, raise the area, do not try to remove venom.

## animal-bite
- Sources read: AHA-FA 9.3; ARC-LAY Animal Bites; CDC-RAB (five pages); MLP Animal bites - self-care and Human bites - self-care; wound-care.json (for consistency with its bite step and WMS irrigation).
- Numbers checked: 3 to 5 minutes — rinse the bite — MLP 000734 and 000736 (timer 300 s).
- Numbers checked: 24 hours — see a provider for any animal bite that breaks the skin — MLP 000734.
- Numbers checked: 5 years — tetanus shot — MLP 000734 and 000736.
- Numbers checked: about half a soda can — blood loss needing emergency care — ARC-LAY.
- Numbers checked: at least 7 in 10 — US rabies deaths from bats — CDC About Rabies; except Hawaii — wildlife rabies; nearly 100% — PEP effectiveness; weeks to months — incubation.
- Numbers checked: a few days — delay suturing in unvaccinated people — CDC Yellow Book.
- Numbers checked: at least 1 litre — wilderness irrigation volume — WMS 2014 via wound-care.json (not re-opened in Task 13; checked in Task 11).
- Notes: AHA 9.3 (1, C-EO): bites and saliva-contaminated wounds evaluated at a medical facility as soon as possible; early antibiotics appear to help high-risk hand bites (supportive text). CDC: wash immediately with soap and water; povidone-iodine or other virucidal agent if available; prompt care but not ER unless the wound needs it; PEP before symptoms, regardless of the interval; any bat contact assessed unless a bite is definitively ruled out; bat contact with bare skin or in hair is a possible bite (Yellow Book); trap a bat only if safe, do not release it before talking to public health. ARC-LAY: stray or wild animal bite marked for immediate emergency treatment; call 911 for a possibly rabid animal; remember the animal's appearance, behaviour and location; rabies signs in animals. MLP: do not try to catch the animal; contact animal control; do not put your mouth on a wound; human bites on head, face, neck, hands, fingers or feet need a provider right away. The mauling screen (s4) asks about deep bites on neck, chest or belly, not any bite there.

## poisoning
- Sources read: ARC-LAY Poison Exposure; AHA-FA 8.1, 8.2, 8.3; PC First aid: Act fast!, Ipecac, Activated charcoal; MLP Poisoning first aid.
- Numbers checked: 15 minutes — eye irrigation with copious tap water — AHA-FA 8.3 (1, C-LD); at least 15 to 20 minutes, room temperature — PC. After review I3 poisoning has no eye timer: s6 starts the rinse and sends to the Eye injury protocol (its 900 s timer).
- Numbers checked: at least 15 minutes — skin irrigation with running water — AHA-FA 8.2 (1, B-NR) and PC (timer 900 s).
- Numbers checked: an hour after irrigation started — persisting eye symptoms need an urgent eye exam — PC.
- Numbers checked: within 10 minutes / within 3 minutes — early skin irrigation associated with fewer full-thickness burns — AHA-FA 8.2 supportive text.
- Numbers checked: 1-800-222-1222 — Poison Help — ARC-LAY, MLP, PC. 55 regional centers — ARC-LAY (not used in the app).
- Numbers checked: younger than 5 — children most at risk — ARC-LAY.
- Notes: ARC-LAY: move away from the source if safe; CPR if unresponsive and not breathing; recovery position if not fully awake but breathing; if alert call Poison Help; do not give food or drink unless advised; do not induce vomiting; save vomit if poison unknown; find type, amount, time, weight; asterisked emergency signs. PC: 911 for collapse, seizure, trouble breathing or cannot be woken; small water or milk only for burning/irritating/caustic products in a conscious, non-convulsing person able to swallow; ipecac no longer recommended, not made or sold; do not treat with activated charcoal at home (most poison centers do not recommend keeping it). MLP: left side; wipe the mouth with a cloth-wrapped finger; save vomit from a plant; no neutralising with lemon juice or vinegar; do not wait for symptoms; no matches or lighters near gas; get care after fumes even if fine. AHA 8.2: brush off dry chemical first (2a); remove clothing and jewellery (1); hydrofluoric acid and phenol need other agents; dry sodium hydroxide and elemental sodium or potassium may react with water. AHA 8.1: CPR with ventilations and naloxone for unresponsive, not breathing normally suspected opioid overdose (1, B-NR). Carbon monoxide goes to the Carbon monoxide poisoning protocol; opioid overdose to the Opioid overdose protocol (next batch).

## poison-plants
- Sources read: AHA-FA 7.3; CDC-PLANTS; MLP Poison ivy - oak - sumac rash; AAD How to treat the rash.
- Numbers checked: up to 100% immediately, 50% at 10 minutes, 25% at 15 minutes, 10% at 30 minutes — urushiol removed by soap and water — AHA-FA 7.3 supportive text.
- Numbers checked: 55% to 70% — symptom reduction even when washing 2 hours after contact — AHA-FA 7.3 supportive text.
- Numbers checked: within 30 minutes — try to wash — MLP.
- Numbers checked: up to 5 years — urushiol active on objects — CDC-PLANTS.
- Numbers checked: days 4 to 7 — worst symptoms; 1 to 3 weeks — rash duration — MLP. Within a few days — rash appears — CDC-PLANTS.
- Notes: AHA 7.3: wash with soap and water or a commercial decontaminant as soon as possible (1, B-NR); OTC topical steroids uncertain (2b); cool compresses (2b); oatmeal baths (2b); OTC antihistamines uncertain (2b). CDC-PLANTS: rinse immediately with rubbing alcohol, poison plant wash or dishwashing soap and lots of water; rinse often; scrub under nails; wet compresses, calamine, hydrocortisone, oatmeal; no creams or lotions on broken skin; diphenhydramine may cause drowsiness; care for severe cases or face/genitals; 911 or ER for severe allergic reaction; blister fluid does not spread; burning plants cause lung irritation; wash clothing separately in hot water. MLP: emergency care for severe allergic reaction or smoke exposure; provider for uncontrolled itch, face, lips, eyes, genitals, infection signs. AAD: ER list (trouble breathing or swallowing; rash around eyes, mouth or genitals; facial swelling; worsening itch or no sleep; rash over most of the body; fever); no antihistamine on the skin; leave blisters alone; do not scratch.

## Task 13 path check
Every path through each protocol was listed with a script: snakebite 4, tick 4, stings 6, spider-scorpion 6, animal-bite 7, poisoning 27 (before the fix; same count after), poison-plants 4. Every protocol screens unresponsive-and-not-breathing-normally and severe allergic signs before its first treatment instruction (snakebite after moving away from the snake, animal-bite after making sure the animal cannot attack again, poisoning after scene safety, with an extra not-fully-awake screen). Each instruction was read against every casualty who can reach it. Changes made by the check:
- poisoning: added s4b (trouble breathing, a seizure, or getting worse: get help now, with the 911 wording) before the eye and skin rinses, so an alert casualty with breathing trouble is not held for 15 minutes of rinsing before the 911 instruction (which had been only in s14).
- poisoning s23 (carbon monoxide pointer): detail now says to come back for a swallowed poison or skin or eye contamination and to call Poison Control, because a casualty can reach s23 after the swallowed branch.
- poisoning s8: labelled group judgement on rinsing in the cold (dry and warm them afterwards).
- poisoning s15, s20: removed unsourced seizure and face-wiping instructions found on re-reading; the seizure line now cites the Red Cross emergency sign and the Recovery position protocol.
- animal-bite s4: the mauling screen was narrowed from "bites on the neck, chest or belly" to "a deep bite or puncture on the neck, chest or belly", so a scratch on the belly is not sent to the Trauma check.
- animal-bite: removed a dummy question that had been used only to jump from the rabies branch to the tetanus step; the human-bite step now carries its own tetanus and infection advice and ends.
- snakebite s3: the allergy screen no longer includes plain "trouble breathing", so a coral snake casualty with weak breathing and no swelling or hives is not sent to the Anaphylaxis protocol; the detail explains.
- stings: the eyeball-sting question (s5) comes before stinger removal (s6), so nobody is told to scrape or pluck a stinger from the eyeball; s22 detail now tells the user to wash and cool other stings.
- stings s8 and s20: removed two unsourced details (dark urine; second rescuer giving epinephrine during CPR).
Every by-mouth step (stings s11, spider-scorpion s13, poison-plants s7) carries the exclusions in its warning; poisoning s10 gives nothing by mouth unless Poison Control advises it; snakebite gives no pain medicine (CDC).
